# The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: $`\Psi`$-Samplers

Justin Deschenaux ^(†)^(†)thanks: Correspondence to
justin.deschenaux@epfl.ch and ssahoo@cs.cornell.edu Affiliation: EPFL,
Lausanne, Switzerland    Caglar Gulcehre Affiliation: EPFL, Lausanne,
Switzerland Affiliation: Microsoft AI    Subham Sekhar
Sahoo¹¹footnotemark: 1 Affiliation: Cornell Tech, NY

###### Abstract

Uniform-state discrete diffusion models excel at few-step generation and
guidance due to their ability to self-correct, making them preferred
over autoregressive or Masked diffusion models in these settings.
However, their sampling quality plateaus with ancestral samplers as the
number of steps increases. We introduce a family of Predictor-Corrector
(PC) samplers for discrete diffusion that generalize prior methods and
apply to arbitrary noise processes. When paired with uniform-state
diffusion, our samplers outperform ancestral sampling on both language
and image modeling, achieving lower generative perplexity at matched
unigram entropy on OpenWebText and better FID/IS scores on CIFAR10.
Crucially, unlike conventional samplers, our PC methods continue to
improve with more sampling steps. Taken together, these findings call
into question the assumption that Masked diffusion is the inevitable
future of diffusion-based language modeling. Beyond sampling, we develop
a memory-efficient curriculum for the Gaussian relaxation training
phase, reducing training time by 25% and memory by 33% compared to Duo
while maintaining comparable perplexity on OpenWebText and LM1B and
strong downstream performance. We release code, checkpoints, and a
video-tutorial on:

[https://s-sahoo.com/duo-ch2](https://s-sahoo.github.io/duo-ch2/)

|     |
| --- |
|     |

Figure 1: Performance on Language Modeling and Image Modeling.
$`\Psi`$-samplers generalize ReMDM ([Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)) to
arbitrary noise distributions. (Left): Generative perplexity (Gen. PPL;
$`\downarrow`$) as a function of NFEs, with nucleus sampling $`p=0.9`$.
$`\Psi`$-samplers consistently improve with more steps, unlike ancestral
sampling which plateaus. Curves are annotated with the average unigram
entropy per sequence as a proxy for diversity. (Right): On CIFAR-10,
$`\Psi`$-samplers achieve better FID ($`\downarrow`$) than MDLM (with
ReMDM).

## 1 Introduction

Diffusion models are powerful generative algorithms that have achieved
remarkable success in modeling continuous data domains, including images
([Ho et al., 2020a](#bib.bib67); [Rombach et al., 2022](#bib.bib68)),
audio ([Kong et al., 2021](#bib.bib69); [Liu et al., 2023b](#bib.bib70);
[Huang et al., 2023](#bib.bib43)), and videos ([Ho et al.,
2022](#bib.bib71); [Esser et al., 2023](#bib.bib72); [Blattmann et al.,
2023](#bib.bib73); [Polyak et al., 2025](#bib.bib42)). Recent advances
have extended diffusion models to categorical data, demonstrating their
potential for language modeling ([Austin et al., 2023](#bib.bib16); [Lou
et al., 2024](#bib.bib28); [Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Shi et
al., 2025](#bib.bib17); [Ou et al., 2025](#bib.bib57); [Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7); [Sahoo et al., 2025b](#bib.bib63)), graphs ([Liu et
al., 2023a](#bib.bib75)), molecules ([Lee et al., 2025](#bib.bib74)),
and audio ([Ku et al., 2025](#bib.bib78)). Unlike autoregressive models
that generate tokens sequentially from left to right, diffusion language
models can decode tokens in parallel and in any order while leveraging
bidirectional contextual information. This capability enables the design
of language models that can be significantly faster than their
autoregressive counterparts while maintaining strong downstream
performance ([Song et al., 2025](#bib.bib76); [Labs et al.,
2025](#bib.bib77)).

Discrete diffusion models primarily employ one of two noise
distributions: a uniform prior or a masked prior that concentrates all
probability mass on a special \[mask\] token. Unlike _Masked Diffusion
Models_ (MDMs), which update each token exactly once, _Uniform-State
Diffusion Models_ (USDMs) allow tokens to be revised multiple times
during generation, enabling self-correction. This makes USDMs
particularly effective for few-step ([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7))
and guided generation ([Schiff et al., 2025](#bib.bib10)). However, the
generation quality of USDMs has not yet matched that of MDMs in
high-sampling-step regimes, and USDMs’ modeling capacity, as measured by
likelihood, remains inferior to MDMs’. Although [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7) proposed a curriculum learning strategy ([Bengio et
al., 2009](#bib.bib24)) that narrows the likelihood gap, this curriculum
is computationally expensive.

To address MDMs’ inability to remask tokens, ReMDM ([Wang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib9)) introduced “Predictor-Corrector” (PC) samplers that
generalize and outperform earlier PC methods ([Campbell et al.,
2022](#bib.bib49); [Gat et al., 2024](#bib.bib47)). These samplers
substantially improve the inference time scaling behavior of MDMs.
However, PC methods for uniform-state diffusion remain underexplored.
[Campbell et al. (2022)](#bib.bib49) proposed PC methods for samplers
that take advantage of the rate change matrices of the continuous-time
Markov chain (CTMC) formulation of discrete diffusion processes, but
such samplers are known to perform worse than ancestral samplers ([Lou
et al., 2024](#bib.bib28); [Schiff et al., 2025](#bib.bib10)).

We propose $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ to address these challenges, which
expands the design space of USDMs using non-Markovian superposition
posteriors (or as we refer to them in this paper, $`\Psi`$-posteriors).
These posteriors align with the intermediate marginals of discrete
diffusion processes and give rise to $`\Psi`$-samplers with
predictor-corrector capabilities that are crucial for improving sample
quality. In addition, $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ introduces an efficient
curriculum learning strategy that advances the approach of [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7) by accelerating training and reducing memory usage.

In summary, our contributions are threefold: (1) we propose a family of
non-Markovian posteriors ($`\Psi`$-posteriors) for discrete diffusion
with arbitrary noise priors that share the same marginals as the
Markovian discrete diffusion process (Sec.
[3](#S3 "3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
(2) We demonstrate that the induced $`\Psi`$-samplers improve text and
image generation quality and scale better than standard ancestral
samplers in high NFE regimes, closing the performance gap with respect
to MDMs coupled with remasking samplers in high NFE regimes for text
generation (Sec.
[5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and surpassing them on image generation tasks (Sec.
[5.1.2](#S5.SS1.SSS2 "5.1.2 Image Modeling ‣ 5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
(3) We reformulate the curriculum learning strategy proposed in [Sahoo
et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7), achieving a 2$`\times`$ speedup while
reducing peak memory usage by 33% and end-to-end training time by 25%,
while maintaining similar perplexity (Fig.
[1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
right, Table
[5](#A4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and downstream task accuracy (Table
[1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Step 2: Approximating the softmax ‣ 4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

## 2 Background

##### Notation

Let
$`\mathcal{V}:=\{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{K}:\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbf{v}_{i}=1\}`$
denote the set of one-hot encodings of discrete random variables over
$`K`$ categories. Let $`{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}^{L}`$ denote a
sequence of $`L`$ discrete variables in $`\mathcal{V}`$ and
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}`$ denote the
entry $`\ell^{\text{th}}`$ in $`{\mathbf{x}}`$. We use boldface to
denote both individual vectors and sequences; the context will make
clear whether a symbol refers to a vector or a sequence. Let $`\Delta`$
denote the $`K`$ simplex. For $`\mathbf{v}\in\Delta`$, let
$`\text{Cat}(\cdot;\mathbf{v})`$ denote a categorical distribution such
that $`\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{u}_{i}=1)=\mathbf{v}_{i}`$, for
$`\mathbf{u}\sim\text{Cat}(\cdot;\mathbf{v}),\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{V}`$.
Let $`\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\rangle`$ and
$`\mathbf{a}\odot\mathbf{b}`$ denote the dot and Hadamard products
between two vectors respectively. Let $`{\bm{1}}=\{1\}^{K}`$ denote the
all-ones vector. Let $`\bm{\pi}\in\Delta`$ be a designated categorical
distribution referred to as the prior.

### 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models

Consider the clean data sequence $`{\mathbf{x}}`$ of length $`L`$ drawn
from the data distribution $`q_{\text{data}}`$. Discrete diffusion
models ([Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015](#bib.bib14); [Austin et al.,
2023](#bib.bib16)) define a sequence of increasingly noisy distributions
$`(q_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}`$, interpolating from $`q_{\text{data}}`$ to a
factorized prior distribution, which is a product of $`L`$ independent
$`\text{Cat}(.;\bm{\pi})`$ distributions, using Markovian transitions
defined independently across input dimensions ([Campbell et al.,
2022](#bib.bib49); [Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Shi et al.,
2025](#bib.bib17); [Ou et al., 2025](#bib.bib57); [Schiff et al.,
2025](#bib.bib10); [Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)). Let
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}\sim\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
denote the intermediate latents (sequence) at time step $`t`$. This work
focuses on factorized, interpolating noise processes ([Sahoo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib27)), whose conditional marginal distribution takes the
form:

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell};{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}})=\text{Cat}(.;{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}+(1-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}})\bm{\pi}),
``` |  | (1) |

where $`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}\in[0,1]`$ is
monotonically decreasing with $`t`$, and is known as the noise schedule.
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
defines the forward process, which progressively corrupts the data. The
goal is to learn a generative process $`{p_{\theta}}`$, parameterized by
a neural network with parameters $`\theta`$, that reverses this forward
process to map from the noise prior back to $`q_{\text{data}}`$. The
model is typically trained by minimizing the “Negative Evidence Lower
Bound” (NELBO). The choice of token prior $`\bm{\pi}`$ gives rise to two
popular variants: Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) and Uniform-state
Diffusion Models (USDMs), which we discuss in the following.

#### 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes

MDMs ([Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Shi et al., 2025](#bib.bib17);
[Ou et al., 2025](#bib.bib57)) use a masked prior, where
$`\bm{\pi}={\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{V}`$ is the one-hot representation of
a special \[mask\] token ([Devlin et al., 2019](#bib.bib21)). During the
forward process
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
tokens either remain unchanged or transition to the masked state
$`{\mathbf{m}}`$, after which they stay masked. This behavior carries
over to the reverse process. The posterior of the reverse process
$`q^{\text{MDM}}_{s|t}`$ for $`0\leq s<t<1`$ can be derived using Bayes’
Rule, and is given by:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q^{\text{MDM}}_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\begin{cases}\text{Cat}\left(.;\frac{\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+\frac{1-\alpha_{s}}{1-\alpha_{t}}{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\right)&\text{if ${\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\mathbf{m}}$,}\\
\text{Cat}(.;{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}`$ |  | (2) |

The approximate reverse posterior is
$`{p^{\theta}_{s|t}}=\prod_{\ell}q^{\text{MDM}}_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}1:L}},t))`$
where $`\mathbf{x}_{\theta}:\mathcal{V}^{L}\times[0,1]\to\Delta^{L}`$ is
the denoising model. A key limitation is that once unmasked, tokens
cannot be remasked
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
This can create compounding errors during inference, as the denoising
model $`\mathbf{x}_{\theta}`$ imperfectly models the clean data.

##### Predictor-Corrector Methods

[Wang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib9) propose posteriors, and associated
samplers (ReMDM) that maintain the same marginals as
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
during the generation process, while allowing remasking and generalizing
previous training-free predictor-corrector methods such as [Campbell et
al. (2022)](#bib.bib49); [Gat et al. (2024)](#bib.bib47).

#### 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes

Alternatively, discrete diffusion models can use a uniform prior
$`\bm{\pi}={\bm{1}}/K`$ ([Schiff et al., 2025](#bib.bib10); [Sahoo et
al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)). This choice allows tokens to change values
multiple times throughout the generative process, in contrast to Masked
diffusion. This property allows USDMs to excel in few-step generation
([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)) and guidance applications ([Schiff et
al., 2025](#bib.bib10)).

USDMs admit the following posterior distribution
$`q^{\text{USDM}}_{s|t}`$ (for brevity, we simply write
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}{q_{s|t}}}`$ for
$`q^{\text{USDM}}_{s|t}`$):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}{q_{s|t}}}(.\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\text{Cat}\left(.;\frac{K{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\odot{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t|s}}-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}){\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}){\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t|s}})(1-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{s}}){\bm{1}}/K}{K{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}\langle{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\rangle+1-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}}\right).
``` |  | (3) |

This posterior induces the following NELBO ([Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{NELBO}\left({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q},p_{\theta};{\mathbf{x}}\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\mathcal{U}[0,1],\,{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}};\alpha_{t})}\;\sum_{\ell\in[L]}\textit{f}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},t),{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}};{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}),`$ |  | (4) |

where

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\textit{f}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},t),`$ | $`\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}};{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}^{\prime}}{K{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}}\Bigg[\frac{K}{\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{r}}-\frac{K}{(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{r}}-\left(\zeta_{t}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}+\mathds{1}_{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\neq{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}\right)\sum_{j}\log\frac{(\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{\theta})_{r}}{(\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{\theta})_{j}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-K\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}}{1-{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}}\log\frac{(\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{\theta})_{r}}{(\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{\theta})_{i}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\neq{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}-\left((K-1)\zeta_{t}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta_{t}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\neq{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}\right)\log\zeta_{t}\Bigg].`$ |  | (5) |

Here,
$`\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}=K\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\alpha_{t}){\bm{1}}`$,
$`\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{\theta}=K\alpha_{t}\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t)+(1-\alpha_{t}){\bm{1}}`$,
$`\alpha_{t}^{\prime}`$ denotes the time derivative of $`\alpha_{t}`$,
$`r=\arg\max_{j\in[K]}({\mathbf{z}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{t})_{j}`$
is the nonzero entry of $`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}`$,
$`\zeta_{t}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{K\alpha_{t}+1-\alpha_{t}}`$, and $`i`$
denotes the index in $`{\mathbf{x}}`$ corresponding to 1, that is,
$`{\mathbf{x}}_{i}=1`$.

##### The Diffusion Duality

[Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) show that USDMs emerge from an
underlying Gaussian diffusion process ([Sohl-Dickstein et al.,
2015](#bib.bib14); [Ho et al., 2020b](#bib.bib8); [Song et al.,
2021](#bib.bib60); [Kingma et al., 2023](#bib.bib22)) on the one-hot
representation
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}`$.
The Gaussian diffusion begins with
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ and
progressively adds Gaussian noise leading to a sequence of noisy latents
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{q}_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
for $`t\in[0,1]`$ with the marginals:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{q}_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}};{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}})={\mathcal{N}}(.;{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},(1-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})\mathbf{I}_{K}),
``` |  |

where
$`({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}})_{t\in[0,1]}`$
is a monotonically decreasing noise schedule. Let
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathcal{V}`$ map a
continuous vector $`\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}`$ to the one-hot vector
corresponding to the index of its largest entry in $`\mathbf{v}`$, that
is,
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}(\mathbf{v})={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}_{{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathcal{V}}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}`$.
When applied to a sequence of Gaussian latents $`{\mathbf{w}}`$,
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}`$ transforms them to the discrete
latents $`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}`$ whose marginals take the form:
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}};{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))`$,
where the function
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}:[0,1]\to[0,1]`$ is the Diffusion
Transformation Operator:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}})=\frac{K}{K-1}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\left(z-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}}{\sqrt{1-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}}\right)\Phi^{K-1}(z)\text{d}z-\frac{1}{K}\right],`$ |  | (6) |

where $`\phi(z)=\exp(-z^{2}/2)/\sqrt{2\pi}`$ and
$`\Phi(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{z}\phi(t){\text{d}}t`$ are the standard Normal
PDF and CDF, respectively. More formally, this relationship is expressed
as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}};{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))=[{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}]_{\scalebox{0.6}{★}}{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{q}_{t}}({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}};{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}})`$ |  | (7) |

where the ★ operator denotes the pushforward of the $`K`$-dimensional
Gaussian density under the $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}`$ map,
yielding a categorical distribution with $`K`$ classes. Note that while
the marginal distribution
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}|{\mathbf{x}};{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))`$
matches the discrete-space marginal in
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
this does not imply that the full trajectory
$`\{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}({\mathbf{w}}_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}`$
follows a (Markovian) discrete diffusion process ([Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)). An interesting outcome of
([7](#S2.E7 "Equation 7 ‣ The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
is that the discrete NELBO
([4](#S2.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
can be written in terms of Gaussian latents in the following manner,
where the second $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}`$ is applied to each
token independently:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\text{NELBO}\left({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q},p_{\theta};{\mathbf{x}}\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}},t\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1],{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{q}_{t}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]}\textit{f}\Big({\mathbf{z}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{t}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}),\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}({{\mathbf{w}}_{t}}),t),{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}});{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\Big).`$ |  | (8) |

Figure 2: $`\Psi`$-samplers combine predictor and corrector steps. The
predictor transitions from $`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}`$ to $`{\mathbf{z}}_{s}`$
via $`{q_{s|t}}`$, but fails to remask tokens in MDMs. The corrector
steps inject noise via $`q_{s}`$, to revise earlier predictions. For
$`\kappa_{t}<1`$, noise injection enables error correction while
preserving the forward process marginals. Our framework extends prior PC
methods ([Campbell et al., 2022](#bib.bib49); [Gat et al.,
2024](#bib.bib47); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)) to arbitrary priors
$`\bm{\pi}`$.

##### Curriculum Learning

Curriculum learning ([Bengio et al., 2009](#bib.bib24)) trains models by
gradually increasing task difficulty. Building on this idea, [Sahoo et
al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) accelerate early training by using a biased but
low-variance NELBO estimator for
([8](#S2.Ex3 "In The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Concretely, during the first 50% of training steps, the hard
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}`$ used to convert Gaussian latents
into discrete tokens in the transformer’s input is replaced by a
low-temperature softmax relaxation. This relaxation interfaces naturally
with the transformer input layer: if the latent at position $`\ell`$ is
a probability vector
$`{\mathbf{y}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\in\Delta^{K}`$,
then the token representation is computed as a matrix product
$`\mathbf{V}^{\top}{\mathbf{y}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$,
where $`\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times m}`$ is the vocabulary
embedding matrix. For one-hot
$`{\mathbf{y}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ (as produced
by $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}`$), this reduces to a standard
embedding lookup; for softmax-relaxed latents, it becomes a linear
combination of vocabulary embeddings. As a result, the model is no
longer asked to denoise from a fully corrupted discrete token embedding,
but instead receives an embedding that is a superposition of clean and
noisy token embedding. This “partially clean” input provides a direct
signal about the underlying token, making denoising easier than relying
solely on the surrounding context. Fig.
[3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Intuitive Explanation ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
(top) illustrates this curriculum. More formally, during the curriculum
phase [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) optimize the following loss,
where the $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}`$ is applied
independently at each position:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{train}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}},t\sim{\mathcal{U}}[\beta,\gamma],{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{q}_{t}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]}\textit{f}\Big({\mathbf{z}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{t}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}({\mathbf{w}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}_{t}),\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}({{\mathbf{w}}_{t}/}{\tau}),t),{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}}:={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}});{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\Big).`$ |  | (9) |

Notice that $`\mathcal{L}^{\text{train}}`$ in
([9](#S2.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ Curriculum Learning ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
reduces to the NELBO
([8](#S2.Ex3 "In The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
in the limit $`\lim_{\tau\to 0}`$, for $`\beta=0`$ and $`\gamma=1`$,
since
$`\lim_{\tau\to 0}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}(\mathbf{v}/\tau)={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\argmax}(\mathbf{v})`$,
as shown by [Jang et al. (2017)](#bib.bib11); [Maddison et al.
(2017)](#bib.bib12). However, explicitly materializing the
high-dimensional latents $`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}`$ is memory-intensive, an
issue we address in Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").

### 2.2 Diffusion Guidance

For continuous data, diffusion models have achieved state-of-the-art
controllable generation through both classifier-based guidance
([Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015](#bib.bib14); [Dhariwal and Nichol,
2021](#bib.bib64)) and Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG; [Nichol and
Dhariwal (2021)](#bib.bib30); [Ho and Salimans (2022)](#bib.bib53)).
These approaches have since been extended to discrete data ([Gruver et
al., 2023](#bib.bib66)). Let $`y\in\{1,\dots,C\}`$ denote one of $`C`$
possible classes. For CFG, the sampling posterior
$`{p_{\theta}}^{(\gamma)}`$, which modulates the strength of the
guidance term via the temperature parameter $`\gamma`$, is defined as
([Nisonoff et al., 2024](#bib.bib65); [Schiff et al.,
2025](#bib.bib10)):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log{p_{\theta}}^{(\gamma)}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid y,{\mathbf{z}}_{t})=\gamma\log{p_{\theta}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid y,{\mathbf{z}}_{t})+(1-\gamma)\log{p_{\theta}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid\emptyset,{\mathbf{z}}_{t});\;\forall\ell\in[L],
``` |  | (10) |

where $`\emptyset`$ denotes no class conditioning, and $`{p_{\theta}}`$
is the generative posterior (Sec.
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

## 3 The $`\Psi`$-Posteriors

Multiple joint distributions can give rise to the same marginals as the
discrete diffusion process defined in
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
In this work, we introduce a family of posteriors, denoted $`{\Psi}`$,
that share the same marginals as in
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"));
see Suppl.
[A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Proof that the Ψ-posteriors have the correct marginals ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for details. These alternative generative processes are non-Markovian
and apply both to the Masked diffusion processes and to the
Uniform-state diffusion processes. Specifically, we define the
posteriors for the generative process as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Psi_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\kappa_{t}{{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})+(1-\kappa_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}});\;\forall\ell\in[L]`$ |  | (11) |

where $`\kappa_{t}\in[0,1]`$ and
$`\Psi_{1}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\text{Cat}(.|{\mathbf{\bm{\pi}}})`$,
with $`\bm{\pi}={\mathbf{m}}`$ for MDMs and $`\bm{\pi}={\bm{1}}/K`$ for
USDMs.
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
is thus a linear combination of the forward process
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and the reverse posteriors
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[3](#S2.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
of standard discrete diffusion models. We therefore refer to these as
superposition posteriors, or simply $`\Psi`$-posteriors.

##### $`\Psi`$-Forward Processes

Consider the interpolating diffusion process in
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
discretized into $`T`$ steps. Let $`{\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}`$ denote the
latent variables at times $`{t(i)}=i/T`$ for $`0\leq i\leq T`$. The
distribution of a trajectory $`{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}`$ factorizes
independently over tokens as:
$`\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}|{\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{\ell}\Psi({\mathbf{z}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}_{0:1}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
where
$`\Psi({\mathbf{z}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}_{0:1}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\Psi_{1}({\mathbf{z}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}_{1}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\prod_{i=1}^{T}\Psi_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}_{s(i)}|{\mathbf{z}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}_{t(i)},{\mathbf{x}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}})`$.
In what follows, we use $`s,t`$ as shorthand for $`{s(i)},{t(i)}`$,
respectively. The forward process can be derived from Bayes’ rule:
$`\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})/\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$.
Unlike the Markovian interpolating process in
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
this forward process is generally not Markovian, since each
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ may
depend on both
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ and
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$.

##### $`\Psi`$-Reverse Processes

In Suppl.
[A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Approximate Reverse Marginals ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we show that the approximate reverse posterior takes the form:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle[\Psi^{\theta}_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t})]^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}=\kappa_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t))+(1-\kappa_{t})\left[\alpha_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{0|t}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\mathbf{x}_{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t))+(1-\alpha_{s})\bm{\pi}\right].`$ |  | (12) |

where $`\mathbf{x}_{\theta}`$ denotes the denoising model. We dub
([12](#S3.E12 "Equation 12 ‣ Ψ-Reverse Processes ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
as $`\Psi`$-sampler. For $`(\kappa_{t}=1)_{t\in[0,1]}`$, we recover the
standard ancestral sampler defined in
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
for MDMs and
([3](#S2.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
for USDMs. Notice that for $`\kappa_{t}<1`$, $`\Psi_{s|t}`$ corresponds
to a noisier version of the ancestral sampler marginal $`{q_{s|t}}`$.
This is analogous to Predictor-Corrector methods in Gaussian diffusion
([Song et al., 2021](#bib.bib60)), where the corrector introduces
additional Gaussian noise. In our case, $`q_{t}`$ plays the role of the
corrector, while $`{q_{s|t}}`$ acts as the predictor. The
$`\Psi`$-posteriors also admit a principled NELBO formulation (see
Suppl.
[A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Negative Evidence Lower Bound ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
though this is not directly relevant for sampling.

##### Corollary

For $`\bm{\pi}={\mathbf{m}}`$, different choices of
$`\{\kappa_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}`$ recover previous Predictor-Corrector
formulations in the literature ([Campbell et al., 2022](#bib.bib49);
[Gat et al., 2024](#bib.bib47); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)) (see
Suppl.
[A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Recovering Predictor-Corrector Methods for Masked Diffusion ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for the proof). The $`\Psi`$ framework thus subsumes these samplers as
special cases, extending these predictor-corrector methods for discrete
diffusion with any prior $`\bm{\pi}`$.

##### Intuitive Explanation

In practice, the denoiser $`\mathbf{x}_{\theta}`$ imperfectly models the
clean data $`{\mathbf{x}}`$. The key to the effectiveness of
$`\Psi`$-sampler is the offset term
$`(1-\kappa_{t})(1-\alpha_{s})\bm{\pi}`$ in
([12](#S3.E12 "Equation 12 ‣ Ψ-Reverse Processes ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
which enables error correction during generation. For MDMs
($`\bm{\pi}={\mathbf{m}}`$), this offset allows previously denoised
tokens to return to the masked state, unlike the ancestral sampler,
which prevents remasking (see Sec.
[2.1.1](#S2.SS1.SSS1 "2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Incorrect tokens can thus be replaced with better ones. For USDMs
($`\bm{\pi}={\bm{1}}/K`$), the offset ensures every token has non-zero
sampling probability. Even if the denoiser assigns near-zero probability
to the correct token, the $`\Psi`$-sampler gives it a chance to appear,
whereas ancestral sampling would not. While this offset may occasionally
introduce incorrect tokens, the marginals of the $`\Psi`$-samplers
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
match those of the Markovian forward process
([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
hence we converge to the correct distribution given sufficient samples.

![Refer to caption](2602.21185v2/efficient_curriculum.png)

Figure 3: Efficient Curriculum for USDMs. Duo ([Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)) replaces discrete lookups with linear combinations of
all $`K`$ embeddings: (1) Gaussian diffusion on one-hot representations,
(2) Low-temperature $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}`$, (3)
weighted sum. $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ exploits the sparsity of the tempered
softmax (most weights are effectively zero), and simulate the k largest
entries (out of K) using ordered statistics. The approximate normalizer
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\tilde{Z}}`$ admits a closed form expression
([14](#S4.E14 "Equation 14 ‣ Step 2: Approximating the softmax ‣ 4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ has a 33% lower memory and 25% faster training than
Duo.

## 4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training

Recall from Sec.
[2.1.2](#S2.SS1.SSS2.Px2 "Curriculum Learning ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
that the curriculum of [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) accelerates
training by replacing discrete tokens as inputs to the transformer with
softmaxed Gaussian latents. Naively, however, this requires
materializing a $`K`$-dimensional weight vector for every token at every
training step, which is infeasible for modern LLM vocabularies with
$`K>100{,}000`$ ([Touvron et al., 2023](#bib.bib3); [OpenAI,
2024](#bib.bib1)). Our key observation is that [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7) use a very low temperature $`\tau=10^{-3}`$ in
([8](#S2.Ex3 "In The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
At such temperatures, the softmax concentrates almost all its mass on
only a few entries, making most of the $`K`$ weights negligible. We
exploit this induced sparsity by approximating the full linear
combination using only $`k\ll K`$ embeddings (Fig.
[3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Intuitive Explanation ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
bottom). We illustrate the process hereby:

##### Step 1: Generating top-$`k`$ entries in $`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ w/o materializing it

Let $`o\in[K]`$ be the nonzero coordinate of the one-hot vector
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$, i.e.
$`({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{o}=1`$.
Recall that
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}\bm{\epsilon}`$,
with
$`{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}=\sqrt{1-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}`$
and $`\bm{\epsilon}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{K})`$.
Therefore,
$`({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{o}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
and
$`({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{i\neq o}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$.
All coordinates in $`[K]\setminus{o}`$ are exchangeable (i.i.d. with
mean $`0`$), while the coordinate $`o`$ is the only one with a shifted
mean. As a result, the top-$`k`$ set falls into one of two cases: Case
1: $`o`$ is not among the top-$`k`$, so all $`k`$ winners lie in
$`[K]\setminus{o}`$. Case 2: $`o`$ is among the top-$`k`$, so the
winners are $`{o}`$ plus $`k-1`$ indices from $`[K]\setminus{o}`$. Next,
we describe how to sample the top-$`k`$ values (and later their indices)
without ever forming the full $`K`$-dimensional vector.

Let $`m=K{-}1`$ and consider $`m`$ i.i.d. samples
$`\tilde{w}_{1},\dots,\tilde{w}_{m}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$.
Rather than drawing all $`m`$ values, we exploit the fact that order
statistics of i.i.d. *uniform* random variables can be sampled
recursively: the maximum of $`m`$ i.i.d. $`\mathcal{U}[0,1]`$ variables
has CDF $`u^{m}`$, so it can be sampled directly. Conditioned on the
maximum, the remaining values are also i.i.d. uniforms, so the
next-largest can be sampled the same way, and so on. Applying the
inverse normal CDF
$`\Phi^{-1}(\cdot)\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}`$
converts the top-$`k`$ uniform order statistics into the top-$`k`$
Gaussian values (see Suppl.
[B.1.4](#A2.SS1.SSS4 "B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for details). We denote the result
$`\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{K}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\mathcal{K}_{k})`$, where
$`\mathcal{K}_{j}`$ is the $`j`$-th largest among the $`K{-}1`$
zero-mean coordinates.

We draw independently the “special” entry
$`\tilde{w}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$,
which matches the distribution of
$`({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{o}`$.
Now compare $`\tilde{w}`$ to the current $`k`$-th largest value among
the zero-mean coordinates: If $`\mathcal{K}_{k}>\tilde{w}`$, then $`o`$
cannot enter the top-$`k`$. We are in Case 1, and $`\mathcal{K}`$
already contains the correct top-$`k`$ values. If
$`\tilde{w}>\mathcal{K}_{k}`$, then $`o`$ must be in the top-$`k`$. We
are in Case 2. Let $`r=\big|\{j\in[k]:\mathcal{K}_{j}>\tilde{w}\}\big|`$
be the number of values in $`\mathcal{K}`$ that are larger than
$`\tilde{w}`$. We insert $`\tilde{w}`$ at rank $`r+1`$ and drop the
previous smallest value:
$`\mathcal{K}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}_{1:r}\|\tilde{w}\|\mathcal{K}_{r+1:k-1}`$,
where $`\|`$ denotes concatenation.

For clarity, let $`\binom{S}{m}`$ denote the set of all possible tuples
with $`m`$ distinct elements in the set $`S`$. We generate the index
tuple $`\mathcal{I}`$ corresponding to the top-$`k`$ values
$`\mathcal{K}`$ as follows: Case 1: all top-$`k`$ indices lie in
$`[K]\setminus{o}`$; hence,
$`\mathcal{I}\sim\binom{[K]\setminus\{o\}}{k}.`$ Case 2: the index $`o`$
appears at position $`r+1`$ in $`\mathcal{K}`$. Sample the indices for
the remaining $`k-1`$ values from $`[K]\setminus{o}`$, split into those
above and below $`o`$: $`\mathcal{I}=L\|(o)\|R`$, where
$`L\sim{\binom{[K]\setminus\{o\}}{r}},R\sim\binom{[K]\setminus\{o\}\setminus L}{k-r-1}`$.
This produces both the top-$`k`$ values and their matching indices while
sampling only $`O(k)`$ random variables, without constructing the full
$`K`$-dimensional vector.

##### Step 2: Approximating the softmax

Given the top-$`k`$ values and indices $`(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{I})`$
from Step 1, we approximate the softmax-weighted embedding vector by
retaining only the $`k`$ selected rows of the
$`\texttt{embeddings}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times d}`$ ($`d`$ denotes the
embedding size):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})^{\top}\texttt{embeddings}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\exp(\mathcal{K}_{i}/\tau)}{\tilde{Z}}\texttt{embeddings}[{\mathcal{I}_{i}}],`$ |  | (13) |

where $`\texttt{embeddings}[j]`$ denotes the $`j`$-th row. The
normalizer $`\tilde{Z}`$ includes both sampled (top-$`k`$) and unsampled
terms. While each unsampled term is small, their sum may be
non-negligible, hence we approximate it as (Suppl.
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tilde{Z}\approx`$ | $`\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{i}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{top-$k$ terms}}+\underbrace{\delta\exp\left(\frac{\tilde{w}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{clean token}}+\underbrace{(K-k-\delta)\exp\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}{2\tau^{2}}-\log\Phi\!\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)+\log\Phi\!\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}/\tau}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)\right)}_{\text{unsampled zero-mean terms}},`$ |  | (14) |

where $`\delta=1`$ if $`\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{K}`$ (case 2) and 0
otherwise. We provide the full derivation in Suppl.
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
and pseudocode in Algo.
[3](#alg3 "Algorithm 3 ‣ B.4 Implementation of the Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").

Lastly, the curriculum objective in
([9](#S2.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ Curriculum Learning ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
requires evaluating the diffusion transformation operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)`$. Directly computing
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ via
([6](#S2.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
is prohibitively expensive during training; [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7) therefore precompute and cache many
$`({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))`$
pairs, which is cumbersome. Instead, we compute
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)`$ on the fly using its
Taylor expansion; see Suppl.
[B.3.1](#A2.SS3.SSS1 "B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").

|                               |       |       |       |       |       |        |       |
|-------------------------------|-------|-------|-------|-------|-------|--------|-------|
|                               | Arc-e | Arc-c | HSwag | WinoG | PIQA  | MathQA | OQA   |
| AR Transformer                | 38.55 | 23.04 | 30.55 | 53.19 | 63.60 | 22.41  | 32.4  |
| MDLM^(†)                      | 34.26 | 24.66 | 31.54 | 51.93 | 57.89 | 20.70  | 28.60 |
| Duo                           | 28.11 | 25.43 | 26.46 | 47.20 | 51.14 | 20.00  | 23.40 |
| $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ ($`k=2`$) | 27.32 | 26.11 | 26.26 | 49.64 | 52.12 | 20.40  | 27.80 |
| $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ ($`k=3`$) | 28.28 | 25.00 | 25.89 | 47.36 | 50.65 | 21.01  | 23.00 |
| $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ ($`k=5`$) | 28.03 | 25.77 | 26.90 | 50.12 | 51.25 | 20.20  | 25.40 |

Table 1: Accuracy on multiple-choice question answering datasets.
Abbreviations: Arc-e (ARC-Easy), Arc-c (ARC-Challenge), HSwag
(HellaSwag), WinoG (Winogrande), PIQA (Physical Intelligence Question
Answering), OQA (OpenBookQA). ^(†)Results from [Deschenaux et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib55). $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ ($`k=2`$) achieves slightly
higher accuracy than Duo on 4 out of 6 tasks. Overall,
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ matches Duo’s performance while using 25% fewer
flops. The highest accuracy among USDMs is bolded. The absolute best per
column is underlined.

## 5 Experiments

We evaluate $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ with $`\Psi`$-samplers on language
modeling (Sec.
[5.1.1](#S5.SS1.SSS1 "5.1.1 Language Modeling ‣ 5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and image generation (Sec.
[5.1.2](#S5.SS1.SSS2 "5.1.2 Image Modeling ‣ 5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
showing that $`\Psi`$-samplers markedly improve text and image quality,
making USDMs outperform MDMs in sample quality. In Sec.
[5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Fast Curriculum ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we show that $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ matches Duo ([Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)) while using 33% less memory and training 25% faster,
enabled by our efficient curriculum strategy (Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

### 5.1 $`\Psi`$-Samplers

#### 5.1.1 Language Modeling

##### Experimental Settings

Figure 4: Illustration of the possible evolution of $`t`$ and the
associated $`\kappa_{t}`$. In practice, we use $`\kappa_{t}`$ close to 1
during the PC phase.

We compare MDLM ([Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27)) and ReMDM ([Wang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib9)) with $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ and $`\Psi`$-samplers.
We use the original checkpoints of [Sahoo et al. (2024)](#bib.bib27),
trained for 1M steps with a batch size of 512 on OpenWebText (OWT;
[Gokaslan and Cohen (2019)](#bib.bib26)) and context length $`L=1024`$.
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ is trained with the same context length, batch size
and number of steps, but with the efficient curriculum. Refer to the
original works for more details. We measure the sample quality using the
Gen. PPL ($`\downarrow`$) computed with GPT-2 Large ([Radford et al.,
2019](#bib.bib38)) and the diversity using the unigram entropy
($`\uparrow`$) ([Dieleman et al., 2022](#bib.bib62); [Sahoo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib27); [Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)). We cast logits to
64-bit precision for sampling ([Zheng et al., 2025](#bib.bib23)). See
Suppl.
[C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix C Experimental Details ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for more details.

##### Results and Ablation

Fig.
[1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
(left) shows the Gen. PPL and entropy as a function of the NFE.
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ with $`\Psi`$-samplers outperforms MDLM with ReMDM
and ancestral sampling across the entire range of NFEs. As the number of
NFEs increases beyond the sequence length, ReMDM and $`\Psi`$-samplers
further improve sample quality while ancestral sampling plateaus. We
ablate on the $`\kappa_{t}`$ schedule type (cap, rescale, loop, see
Suppl.
[A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 ReMDM Sampling Schedules ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
the step-size parameter $`\eta`$, the nucleus sampling threshold
$`p\in\{0.9,0.95,1.0\}`$ (Suppl.
[D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
The rescale schedule with $`\eta=0.05`$ yields the best Gen. PPL while
preserving the unigram entropy. Nucleus sampling ($`p{=}0.9`$)
consistently improves Gen. PPL for both MDLM and Duo, as observed in
[Wang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib9).

##### How to Pick $`\kappa_{t}`$?

We recommend the ReMDM-equivalent rescale schedule with $`\eta=0.05`$
and nucleus sampling ($`p=0.9`$), using the log-linear noise schedule
with linearly decreasing $`t`$, which outperforms the “loop” strategy
(Suppl.
[A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 ReMDM Sampling Schedules ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

##### Experimental Setup

We train the same 35M-parameter U-Net as [Austin et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib16) on CIFAR-10 for 1.5M steps. Following [Schiff et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib10), the U-Net is made class conditional and we sample
with Discrete Classifier-free Guidance (CFG; [Ho and Salimans
(2022)](#bib.bib53); [Schiff et al. (2025)](#bib.bib10)). See Suppl.
[C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix C Experimental Details ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for full training details. We report the *Fréchet Inception Distance*
(FID; [Heusel et al. (2018)](#bib.bib44)) and Inception Score (IS;
[Salimans et al. (2016)](#bib.bib45)) between the training set and
generated samples.

##### Results and Ablation

Fig.
[1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
(right) and Fig.
[6](#A4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
show that $`\Psi`$-samplers and ReMDM substantially improve FID and IS
compared to ancestral sampling, with $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ reaching the
best scores overall. We ablate on the sampling noise schedule (cosine
vs. log-linear), $`\kappa_{t}`$, the activation range
$`[t_{\text{off}},t_{\text{on}}]`$, nucleus sampling, and the
$`\kappa_{t}`$ schedules (including the ReMDM variants; see Suppl.
[A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 ReMDM Sampling Schedules ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Full results are in Suppl.
[D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
Using $`\kappa_{t}`$ close to 1 (light noise injection) with a cosine
schedule achieves the best FID and IS, and $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ tolerates
stronger noise injection than MDLM. With ancestral sampling, nucleus
sampling improves the FID in the low NFE regime for MDLM, and always
helps for Duo. Since it is detrimental to MDLM at high NFE, we do not
use nucleus sampling when using the $`\Psi`$-samplers, for both MDLM and
Duo.

##### How to Pick $`\kappa_{t}`$?

We recommend a cosine sampling schedule with $`\kappa_{t}=0.95`$,
$`t_{\text{on}}\in\{0.5,0.6\}`$, $`t_{\text{off}}=0.1`$ for
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$, and $`\kappa_{t}=0.99`$, $`t_{\text{on}}=1.0`$,
$`t_{\text{off}}=0.1`$ for MDLM. We suggest using a piecewise constant
$`\kappa_{t}`$ with linearly decreasing $`t`$, rather than the ReMDM
loop schedule (Suppl.
[A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 ReMDM Sampling Schedules ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
setting $`\kappa_{t}<1`$ when $`t\in[t_{\text{off}},t_{\text{on}}]`$,
since it outperforms the ReMDM schedules.

### 5.2 Fast Curriculum

##### Experimental Settings

|                                        |      |      |
|----------------------------------------|------|------|
|                                        | LM1B | OWT  |
| Autoregressive                         |      |      |
|    Transformer^(†)                     | 22.3 | 17.5 |
| Masked Diffusion                       |      |      |
|    SEDD Absorb^(†)                     | 32.7 | 24.1 |
|    MDLM^(†)                            | 27.0 | 23.2 |
| Uniform-state Diffusion                |      |      |
|    SEDD Uniform^(†)                    | 40.3 | 29.7 |
|    UDLM^(†)                            | 31.3 | 27.4 |
|    Duo^(†)                             | 29.9 | 25.2 |
|    $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ (Ours), $`k=2`$ | 30.0 | 25.2 |
|    $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ (Ours), $`k=3`$ | 30.1 | 25.3 |
|    $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ (Ours), $`k=5`$ | 30.2 | 25.4 |

Table 2: Test perplexity (PPL) on LM1B and OWT. Lower is better.
^(†)Results from [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7). Best Uniform-state
diffusion numbers are bolded. Duo and $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ achieve
comparable performance across both datasets while requiring 25% fewer
GPU-hours (Table
[4](#A4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
demonstrating the effectiveness of our memory-efficient curriculum.

We train $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ with the scalable curriculum (Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
on OpenWebText (OWT; [Gokaslan and Cohen (2019)](#bib.bib26)) and LM1B
([Chelba et al., 2014](#bib.bib25)). We train all models for 1M steps,
using a batch size of 512. For LM1B, we use the bert-base-uncased
tokenizer with a context length of 128, padding shorter sequences. This
setup follows previous work ([Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Lou et
al., 2024](#bib.bib28); [He et al., 2022](#bib.bib32)). For OWT, we use
the GPT-2 tokenizer ([Radford et al., 2019](#bib.bib38)), and reserve
the last 100k documents for validation, following [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7); [Sahoo et al. (2024)](#bib.bib27). We follow [Lou
et al. (2024)](#bib.bib28) and use a modified diffusion transformer
(DiT) ([Peebles and Xie, 2023](#bib.bib39)) with rotary positional
encoding ([Su et al., 2023](#bib.bib40)). We evaluate the impact of
$`k=\{2,3,5\}`$ during the efficient curriculum. All models are trained
on 16 H100 GPUs with bfloat16 precision. Training uses the loss in
([9](#S2.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ Curriculum Learning ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
with $`\tau=0.001`$ and $`(\beta,\gamma)=(0.03,0.15)`$ for the first
500K steps ([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)).

##### Likelihood Results

Table
[2](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Experimental Settings ‣ 5.2 Fast Curriculum ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows that on both LM1B and OWT, our efficient curriculum
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ matches the performance of Duo with its expensive
curriculum. The lowest validation perplexity is achieved with $`k=2`$,
although $`k\in\{2,3,5\}`$ performs similarly. We also compare the
models trained on OWT in Zero-Shot perplexity, and find that
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ achieves a performance comparable to Duo. That is,
we evaluate on the validation splits of the Penn Treebank ([Marcus et
al., 1993](#bib.bib46)), WikiText ([Merity et al., 2016](#bib.bib50)),
LM1B ([Chelba et al., 2014](#bib.bib25)), LAMBADA ([Paperno et al.,
2016](#bib.bib51)), AG News ([Zhang et al., 2016](#bib.bib52)) and
scientific articles from ArXiv and PubMed ([Cohan et al.,
2018](#bib.bib54)). Table
[5](#A4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows that $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ reaches a zero-shot probability similar
to that of Duo *while requiring 25% less training GPU-hours*.

##### Likelihood-based Downstream Tasks

In Table
[1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Step 2: Approximating the softmax ‣ 4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we compare the multiple-choice question (MCQ) accuracy of Duo,
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$, MDLM ([Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27)), and an
autoregressive transformer (1M training steps with a batch size of 512
on OWT, same hyperparameters as MDLM) using the lm-eval-harness suite
([Gao et al., 2024](#bib.bib56)) ; details in Suppl.
[C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Downstream Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Experimental Details ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
We find that $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ achieves an accuracy similar to that of
Duo, despite requiring 25% less training GPU-hours. However, it trails
MDLM on most tasks, consistent with its higher perplexity.

##### Throughput and Peak Memory Usage

Table
[4](#A4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
reports the throughput and peak memory usage for Duo and
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$. $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ reduces the peak memory usage
by about 33% and doubles the speed of the Curriculum Learning phase.
When applying Curriculum Learning for half of the training steps,
$`\text{Duo}^{++}`$ trains 25% faster than Duo on the 138M-parameter
scale. Notably, both peak memory usage and throughput remain stable over
the full training run when $`k\in\{2,3,5\}`$.

## 6 Related work and Discussion

##### Compatibility with General Discrete Diffusion Processes

This work focuses on discrete diffusion with uniform or masked noise.
However, our approach extends to more general discrete diffusion
processes ([Shaul et al., 2024](#bib.bib20); [von Rütte et al.,
2025](#bib.bib18); [Holderrieth et al., 2025](#bib.bib19)) featuring a
combination of masked and uniform prior, since we provide a general
predictor–corrector algorithm for discrete diffusion with arbitrary
noise.

##### Predictor-Corrector Samplers

In the context of Masked diffusion, ReMDM ([Wang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib9)) generalizes previous predictor-corrector methods
([Campbell et al., 2022](#bib.bib49); [Campbell et al.,
2024](#bib.bib48); [Gat et al., 2024](#bib.bib47)) that were based on
Continuous Time Markov Chain formulation of discrete diffusion
processes. Our approach further generalizes ReMDM to support arbitrary
diffusion processes. Unlike [Lezama et al. (2023)](#bib.bib34); [Zhao et
al. (2025)](#bib.bib35); [Liu et al. (2025)](#bib.bib33); [Kim et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib6), who train an additional corrector module, our method
does not introduce additional learned components.

##### Comparison to Other Discrete Diffusion Samplers

[Park et al. (2024)](#bib.bib36) uses noise-adaptive step sizes; while
we use uniform steps, $`\Psi`$-samplers support any step-size schedule.
[Ren et al. (2025)](#bib.bib37) develops higher-order samplers; we use
only first-order information, though the posterior in
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
could be approximated with higher-order methods. Thus, $`\Psi`$-samplers
complement both lines of work.

## 7 Conclusion

We introduced a unified and practical framework for predictor-corrector
sampling in discrete diffusion language models through
$`\Psi`$-posteriors. By linearly superposing the forward and reverse
diffusion processes
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
the *$`\Psi`$-posteriors* preserve the marginals of standard diffusion
models. Importantly, the *$`\Psi`$-posteriors* and associated
$`\Psi`$-samplers subsume prior masked-diffusion PC samplers ([Campbell
et al., 2022](#bib.bib49); [Gat et al., 2024](#bib.bib47); [Wang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib9)) as special cases, and naturally extend to discrete
diffusion models with uniform prior. Empirically, $`\text{Duo}^{++}`$
with $`\Psi`$-samplers matches the performance of MDMs on natural
language generation and achieves stronger FID and IS scores on CIFAR-10.
Moreover, they exhibit superior scaling: performance continues to
improve with NFEs, unlike ancestral samplers, which plateau. Finally, we
propose a scalable training curriculum ([Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)) that reduces the peak memory usage by 33% and
shortens the training time by 25%. Concurrently, [Sahoo et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib5) show that Duo surpasses an autoregressive model at
the 1.7B scale on the math and reasoning benchmark (GSM8K). Taken
together, these results challenge the view that Masked diffusion is
categorically the future of diffusion language modeling.

## Appendix A $`\Psi`$-Posteriors

### A.1 Approximate Reverse Marginals

We parameterize the (generative) $`\Psi`$-reverse marginals to have a
similar form as the true posterior
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Therefore, the generative reverse marginals also factorize over the
sequence length. Because
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}1:L}`$ is not available
during sampling, there are two terms in
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
that are intractable. First, we choose to replace the posterior
$`{{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
by
$`{{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$.
Additionally, as we cannot sample from
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
without $`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$, we
replace $`{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}`$ by
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{0|t}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$,
$`\forall\ell\in[L]`$. Replacing these two intractable terms yields our
generative reverse marginals:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Psi^{\theta}_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t})=\kappa_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t))+(1-\kappa_{t})\left[\alpha_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{0|t}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t))+(1-\alpha_{s})\pi\right].`$ |  | (15) |

Note that for the masked posterior
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{0|t}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t))={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t)`$.

### A.2 Proof that the $`\Psi`$-posteriors have the correct marginals

Let
$`\Psi_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$
denote the $`\Psi`$-posteriors defined in
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Let $`s`$ denote $`s(k)=t(k-1)`$ and $`t`$ denote $`t(k)`$. To prove
that the $`\Psi`$-posteriors have the correct marginals, we proceed by
(downwards) induction, similar to [Song et al. (2022)](#bib.bib15).
First, note that
$`\Psi_{s}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$
can be written as a marginalization over
$`\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}`$, for
$`s<t`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi_{s}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\Psi_{t}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\Psi_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}})
``` |  | (16) |

##### Base Case

Let
$`\Psi_{1}({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$
denote the marginal at time $`t=1`$. By definition in
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
$`\Psi_{1}({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})=\text{Cat}(.|\bm{\pi})`$.
Therefore, the $`\Psi`$-posteriors have the correct marginal for
$`t=1`$.

##### Induction hypothesis

Suppose that the $`\Psi`$-posteriors have the correct marginal for a
certain $`t\leq 1`$, that is,
$`\Psi_{t}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})={\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$.

##### Inductive step

Based on the induction hypothesis, we now show that
$`\Psi_{s}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})={\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$,
for $`s(k)=t(k-1)`$. Indeed

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Psi_{s}(.|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$ | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\Psi_{t}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\Psi_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\Psi_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\left[\kappa_{t}q_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})+(1-\kappa_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}\kappa_{t}\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})q_{s|t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})+(1-\kappa_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}\kappa_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})+(1-\kappa_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})={\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{s}}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}).`$ |  |

Specifically, $`(1)`$ hold by
([16](#A1.E16 "Equation 16 ‣ A.2 Proof that the Ψ-posteriors have the correct marginals ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
$`(2)`$ by the induction hypothesis, $`(3)`$ by definition of the
$`\Psi`$-posteriors, $`(4)`$ by distributing
$`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$,
$`(5)`$ by definition of marginal probability (first term), and by
observing that
$`\sum_{\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}|{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=1`$
since $`{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{t}}`$ is normalized. This
concludes the inductive step, and shows that the $`\Psi`$-posteriors
have the correct marginal.

### A.3 Negative Evidence Lower Bound

Let $`{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}`$
denote a reverse trajectory with time indices
$`\{0,\frac{1}{T},\frac{2}{T},\dots,1\}`$ for token $`\ell`$. The joint
distribution of
$`({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$
under the generative model factorizes as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p^{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})=p({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\Psi_{1}({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\prod_{i=1}^{T}\Psi^{\theta}_{s\mid t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}),
``` |  | (17) |

where each pair $`(s(i),t(i))`$ denotes one reverse transition with
$`s(i)<t(i)`$. The marginal likelihood is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p^{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}p^{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}},{\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}).
``` |  | (18) |

Introducing the variational distribution
$`\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\Psi_{1}({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\prod_{i=1}^{T}\Psi_{s\mid t}({\mathbf{z}}_{s(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})`$,
Jensen’s inequality results in:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle-\log p^{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{0:1}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})}\big[-\log p({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\big]+\mathrm{KL}\big(\Psi_{1}(\cdot\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\big\|\Psi_{1}\big)`$ |  | (19) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi({\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})}\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(\Psi_{s\mid t}(\cdot\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})\,\big\|\,\Psi^{\theta}_{s\mid t}(\cdot\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t(i)}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\Big)\right].`$ |  | (20) |

This expression is similar to the standard diffusion NELBO, with a
reconstruction term, a prior term at $`t{=}1`$, and a sum of KL
divergences. As $`T\rightarrow\infty`$,
$`p({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$
concentrates around
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$, hence
$`-\log p({\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})\rightarrow 0`$.
Furthermore, the prior term is zero by definition of the
$`\Psi`$-posteriors in
([11](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ 3 The Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

### A.4 Recovering Predictor-Corrector Methods for Masked Diffusion

[Wang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib9) introduce the ReMDM posterior, which
generalizes the MDM posterior
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
by allowing previously decoded tokens to be remasked. For a given
position $`\ell`$, the ReMDM posterior is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
q_{\sigma}({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})=\begin{cases}\text{Cat}\bigl(.;\,(1-\sigma_{t})\,{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+\sigma_{t}\,{\mathbf{m}}\bigr),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}},\\[4.0pt]
\text{Cat}\left(.;\,\frac{\alpha_{s}-(1{-}\sigma_{t})\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\,{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+\frac{1-\alpha_{s}-\sigma_{t}\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\,{\mathbf{m}}\right),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{m}},\end{cases}
``` |  | (21) |

where $`\sigma_{t}\in[0,\,\sigma_{t}^{\max}]`$ is a free parameter that
controls the remasking probability. The upper bound
$`\sigma_{t}^{\max}:=\min\{1,\,(1-\alpha_{s})/\alpha_{t}\}`$ ensures
that
````
