````math
([21](#A1.E21 "Equation 21 ‣ A.4 Recovering Predictor-Corrector Methods for Masked Diffusion ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
defines a valid distribution. When $`\sigma_{t}=0`$, the ReMDM posterior
reduces to the standard MDM posterior. Below, we show that the
$`\Psi`$-posteriors recover the ReMDM posterior with the substitution
$`\kappa_{t}=1-\sigma_{t}/(1-\alpha_{s})`$. Suppose that we work with
Masked diffusion, hence $`\pi={\mathbf{m}}`$. The $`\Psi`$-posteriors
can be expanded as

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\Psi_{s|t}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\kappa_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}{q_{s|t}}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})+(1-\kappa_{t})\left[\alpha_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}q_{0|t}}(.|{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell},{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})+(1-\alpha_{s})\pi\right]`$ |  | (22) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\kappa_{t}\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &\text{Cat}(.;{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}},\\
&\text{Cat}\left(.;\frac{(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}+(\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}){\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\right),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{m}}\end{aligned}\end{cases}+(1-\kappa_{t})\left[\alpha_{s}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}\right]`$ |  | (23) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\kappa_{t}\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &\text{Cat}(.;{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}},\\
&\text{Cat}\left(.;\frac{(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}+(\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}){\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\right),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{m}}\end{aligned}\end{cases}+(1-\kappa_{t})\left[\alpha_{s}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}\right]`$ |  | (24) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\text{Cat}(.;\kappa_{t}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\kappa_{t})[\alpha_{s}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}]),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}}\\
\text{Cat}\left(.;\kappa_{t}\frac{(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}+(\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}){\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}}{1-\alpha_{t}}+(1-\kappa_{t})[\alpha_{s}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}]\right),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{m}}\end{cases}`$ |  | (25) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\text{Cat}(.;[\kappa_{t}+(1-\kappa_{t})\alpha_{s}]{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+(1-\kappa_{t})(1-\alpha_{s}){\mathbf{m}}),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}}\\
\text{Cat}\left(.;\left[\kappa_{t}\frac{\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}+(1-\kappa_{t})\alpha_{s}\right]{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+\left[\kappa_{t}\frac{1-\alpha_{s}}{1-\alpha_{t}}+(1-\kappa_{t})(1-\alpha_{s})\right]{\mathbf{m}}\right),&{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{m}}\end{cases},`$ |  | (26) |

where $`(1)`$ holds since
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}\neq{\mathbf{m}}`$
implies that
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}={\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$,
since in Masked diffusion, the latents
$`{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}`$ are either
a clean token or the masked token.

To conclude, if we pick
$`\kappa_{t}=1-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{1-\alpha_{s}}`$, where $`\sigma_{t}`$
is the free parameter in the ReMDM sampler, then the equation reduces to
the ReMDM posterior. Therefore, the $`\Psi`$-posteriors generalize
ReMDM, which itself generalized the FB ([Campbell et al.,
2022](#bib.bib49)) and DFM ([Gat et al., 2024](#bib.bib47)) posteriors.
Additionally, the $`\Psi`$-posteriors are not limited to Masked
diffusion, as we showed in this work. In Table
[11](#A4.T11 "Table 11 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we sample from the ReMDM-equivalent $`\Psi`$-samplers, comparing the
official ReMDM implementation and our version, and find obtain similar
performance.

### A.5 ReMDM Sampling Schedules

[Wang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib9) introduce three schedules for the
remasking parameter $`\sigma_{t}`$, which controls how aggressively
previously generated tokens are remasked. As shown in Suppl.
[A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Recovering Predictor-Corrector Methods for Masked Diffusion ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
the $`\Psi`$-samplers recover ReMDM when
$`\kappa_{t}=1-\sigma_{t}/(1-\alpha_{s})`$, and
$`(\sigma_{t})_{t=0}^{1}`$ denotes the ReMDM noise injection schedule.
Therefore, each ReMDM $`\sigma_{t}`$ schedule has a direct
$`\Psi`$-samplers equivalent. Below, we present the three $`\sigma_{t}`$
schedules studied in [Wang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib9). The schedules must
satisfy
$`0\leq\sigma_{t}\leq\sigma_{t}^{\max}:=\min\{1,\,(1-\alpha_{s})/\alpha_{t}\}`$
to ensure that the reverse posterior remains a valid distribution, and
are generally defined in terms of $`\sigma_{t}^{\max}`$ and a parameter
$`\eta\in[0,1]`$.

##### Cap Schedule

With the “Cap” schedule, the remasking probability is capped at a
constant $`\eta\in[0,1]`$, as long as it remains in the valid bounds:

|     |                                                        |     |      |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \sigma_{t}=\min\bigl\{\eta,\,\sigma_{t}^{\max}\bigr\}.
       ```                                                     |     | (27) |

##### Rescale Schedule

With the “Rescale” schedule, $`\sigma_{t}`$ is obtained by scaling the
upper bound $`\sigma_{t}^{\max}`$ by a constant $`\eta\in[0,1]`$:

|     |                                        |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \sigma_{t}=\eta\cdot\sigma_{t}^{\max}.
       ```                                     |     | (28) |

##### Loop Schedule

Unlike the cap and rescale schedules, which only modulate
$`\sigma_{t}`$, the “Loop” schedule also changes the evolution of $`t`$
during sampling (see Fig.
[4](#S5.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Experimental Settings ‣ 5.1.1 Language Modeling ‣ 5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for an illustration). It is controlled by the time boundaries
$`t_{\text{on}}>t_{\text{off}}`$. The noise schedule $`\alpha_{t}`$ is
reparametrized to be piecewise linear. First, $`\alpha_{t}`$ increases
linearly from $`0`$ to $`\alpha_{t_{\text{on}}}`$ when
$`t>t_{\text{on}}`$. Secondly, when
$`t\in[t_{\text{off}},\,t_{\text{on}}]`$, it is held constant to
$`\alpha_{t_{\text{on}}}`$, and finally increases from
$`\alpha_{t_{\text{on}}}`$ to $`1`$ when $`t<t_{\text{off}}`$. When
$`t\notin[t_{\text{off}},\,t_{\text{on}}]`$, ReMDM samples from the MDM
posterior
([2](#S2.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 2.1.1 Masked Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
When $`t\in[t_{\text{off}},\,t_{\text{on}}]`$, since
$`\alpha_{t}=\alpha_{s}=\alpha_{t_{\text{on}}}`$, the model samples from
the ReMDM posterior
([21](#A1.E21 "Equation 21 ‣ A.4 Recovering Predictor-Corrector Methods for Masked Diffusion ‣ Appendix A Ψ-Posteriors ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
with a constant $`\sigma_{t}=\eta`$. In practice, $`t_{\text{on}}`$ is
chosen so that $`\alpha_{t_{\text{on}}}`$ is close to $`1`$, where most
tokens have already been decoded.

## Appendix B Fast Curriculum

In this section, we expand on the implementation of the efficient
curriculum. In Sec.
[B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Implementation of the Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we present the overall design, pseudocode, and the three main
implementation challenges. Our approach relies on several mathematical
results, which we present in order of dependency. We first recall
inverse transform sampling (Sec.
[B.1.1](#A2.SS1.SSS1 "B.1.1 Inverse Transform Sampling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
then derive the distributions of the largest (Sec.
[B.1.2](#A2.SS1.SSS2 "B.1.2 Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and second largest (Sec.
[B.1.3](#A2.SS1.SSS3 "B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
uniform order statistics. These results enable generating the $`k`$
largest Gaussian random variables out of $`K`$ without materializing the
full vector (Sec.
[B.1.4](#A2.SS1.SSS4 "B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
We also derive a closed-form expression for the conditional mean of the
exponential of a Gaussian random variable (Sec.
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
used to estimate the softmax normalizer.

Furthermore, although the efficient curriculum could be implemented
using the original definition of the Diffusion Transformation Operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$, we show that
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ admits a convenient series
expansion in Sec.
[B.3.1](#A2.SS3.SSS1 "B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
This avoids the need to precompute 100k function values, and simplifies
the implementation. Finally, in Sec.
[B.3.2](#A2.SS3.SSS2 "B.3.2 Polynomial Approximation of 𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we show that $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ can be well
approximated by a degree-$`9`$ polynomial, which removes the need to
store a large number of coefficients during training.

### B.1 Sampling the top-$`k`$ values in $`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ Without Materializing The Full Vector

Computing our curriculum step requires access to the $`k`$ largest
entries of the diffused weight vector
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}`$,
but explicitly materializing
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ (and then
running a full top-$`k`$) is prohibitively expensive when $`K`$ is
large. In this subsection, we show how to obtain the top-$`k`$ values
and their associated indices while using only $`\mathcal{O}(k)`$ memory
and without simulating all $`K`$ random variables. The key idea is to
decouple *values* from *locations*: we first sample the top-$`k`$
Gaussian order statistics directly via inverse transform sampling (Sec.
[B.1.1](#A2.SS1.SSS1 "B.1.1 Inverse Transform Sampling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
leveraging closed-form expressions for uniform order statistics (Sec.
[B.1.2](#A2.SS1.SSS2 "B.1.2 Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
Sec.
[B.1.3](#A2.SS1.SSS3 "B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and a numerically stable log-space implementation (Algo.
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Sampling The 𝑘^"th"-largest ‣ B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
We then assign these top-$`k`$ to their corresponding indices (Sec.
[B.1.5](#A2.SS1.SSS5 "B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
Algo.
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
This yields an efficient routine whose cost scales with $`k`$ rather
than $`K`$, enabling top-$`k`$ truncation of
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ without
ever materializing it.

#### B.1.1 Inverse Transform Sampling

The Inverse Transform Sampling method ([Devroye, 1986](#bib.bib2)) is an
algorithm for generating a continuous random variables $`X`$ with a
known Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF) $`F_{X}`$. Implementing
Inverse Transform Sampling requires access to the inverse CDF
$`F^{-1}_{X}`$, and a source of $`i.i.d`$ uniform random variables. If
$`X=F^{-1}_{X}(U)`$, where $`U\sim\mathcal{U}[0,1]`$, then
$`X\sim F_{X}`$. Indeed, for $`x\in\mathbb{R}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}(X\leq x)=\mathbb{P}(F^{-1}_{X}(U)\leq x)=\mathbb{P}(U\leq F_{X}(x))=F_{X}(x),
``` |  | (29) |

since for $`a\in[0,1]`$, $`\mathbb{P}(U\leq a)=a`$. This shows that
$`X`$ has the correct distribution.

#### B.1.2 Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of $`K`$

The distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of $`K`$
admits a simple closed-form expression:

###### Proposition B.1 (Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of $`K`$).

$`U^{(1)}\geq U^{(2)}\geq...\geq U^{(K)}`$ denote an order statistic
over $`K`$ i.i.d uniform random variables $`\mathcal{U}([0,\theta])`$
with Cumulative Density Function (CDF) $`F_{U}`$. Suppose that
$`u\in[0,1]`$, then $`F_{U}(u)=\frac{u}{\theta}`$. Then, the CDF
$`F_{U^{(1)}}`$ and probability density function (PDF) $`f_{U^{(1)}}`$
of the largest random variable $`U^{(1)}`$ are as follows:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle F_{U^{(1)}}(u)`$ | $`\displaystyle=F_{U}^{K}(u)=u^{K}\theta^{-K}`$ |  | (30) |
|  | $`\displaystyle f_{U^{(1)}}(u)`$ | $`\displaystyle=KF_{U}^{K-1}(u)f_{U}(u)=Ku^{K-1}\theta^{-K}`$ |  |  |

###### Proof.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{U^{(1)}}(u)=\mathbb{P}(U^{(1)}\leq u)=\mathbb{P}(U_{i}\leq u)_{\forall i\in[K]}=P(U\leq u)^{K}=F_{U}^{K}(u).
``` |  | (31) |

The PDF is obtained by differentiation:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{U^{(1)}}(u)=\frac{d}{du}F_{U^{(1)}}(u)=KF_{U}^{K-1}(u)f_{U}(u),
``` |  | (32) |

∎

#### B.1.3 Distribution of the $`k`$ largest uniform random variable out of $`K`$

In this part, we show how to sample the $`k`$ largest uniform random
variables out of $`K`$. Importantly, we do not need to draw all $`K`$
values and sort them, which would be impractical for large $`K`$. Let
$`U^{(1)}\geq\cdots\geq U^{(K)}`$ denote the order statistics of $`K`$
i.i.d. $`\mathcal{U}[0,1]`$ random variables. We argue that for
$`1\leq i<K`$, conditioned on $`U^{(i)}=u^{(i)}`$, $`U^{(i+1)}`$ is
distributed as the largest out of $`K-i`$ uniform random variables on
$`[0,u^{(i)}]`$. This enables an iterative scheme to generate the $`k`$
largest variables in decreasing order. The argument relies on two
standard results: the conditional density formula (Prop.
[B.2](#A2.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition B.2 (Conditional Density ( , )). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and the joint density of a pair of order statistics (Prop.
[B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition B.3 (Joint Density of Order Statistics ( ( ) ; proof in ( ) )). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
The conditional distribution of $`U^{(i+1)}\mid U^{(i)}=u^{(i)}`$ is
given in Prop.
[B.4](#A2.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition B.4 (Conditional Distribution of 𝑈^(𝑖+1) given 𝑈^(𝑖)). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").

###### Proposition B.2 (Conditional Density ([Berger and Casella, 2001](#bib.bib59))).

Let $`X,Y`$ be two random variables with joint density $`f_{X,Y}`$ and
marginals $`f_{X},f_{Y}`$. Then, the conditional density of $`X`$ given
$`Y=y`$ is

|     |                                               |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       f_{X|Y=y}(x|y)=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{f_{Y}(y)}.
       ```                                            |     | (33) |

###### Proposition B.3 (Joint Density of Order Statistics ([Berger and Casella (2001)](#bib.bib59); proof in [Border (2021)](#bib.bib58))).

Let $`X^{(1)}\geq\cdots\geq X^{(K)}`$ denote the order statistics of
$`K`$ random variables with CDF $`F`$ and PDF $`f`$, arranged in
descending order. Then, the joint density of $`X^{(n)}`$ and
$`X^{(m)}`$, where $`n<m`$ (so that $`X^{(n)}\geq X^{(m)}`$), is given
by

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle f_{X^{(n)},X^{(m)}}(u,v)=`$ |  | (34) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\frac{K!}{(n-1)!(m-n-1)!(K-m)!}\,(1-F(u))^{n-1}\,(F(u)-F(v))^{m-n-1}\,F(v)^{K-m}\,f(u)\,f(v).`$ |  |  |

###### Proposition B.4 (Conditional Distribution of $`U^{(i+1)}`$ given $`U^{(i)}`$).

Let $`U^{(1)}\geq\cdots\geq U^{(K)}`$ denote the order statistics of
$`K`$ independent and uniformly distributed random variables on
$`[0,1]`$, arranged in descending order. For any $`1\leq i<K`$,
conditioned on $`U^{(i)}=u^{(i)}`$, $`U^{(i+1)}`$ is distributed as the
largest of $`K{-}i`$ i.i.d. uniform random variables on $`[0,u^{(i)}]`$.

###### Proof.

From Prop.
[B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition B.3 (Joint Density of Order Statistics ( ( ) ; proof in ( ) )). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
with $`k=i`$ and $`l=i+1`$, the joint density of $`(U^{(i)},U^{(i+1)})`$
for $`\mathcal{U}[0,1]`$ variables (where $`F(u)=u`$ and $`f(u)=1`$) is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{U^{(i)},U^{(i+1)}}(u^{(i)},u^{(i+1)})=\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,(K-i-1)!}\,(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}\,(u^{(i+1)})^{K-i-1},
``` |  | (35) |

since $`(F(u^{(i)})-F(u^{(i+1)}))^{l-k-1}=1`$ as $`l-k=1`$.

To apply Prop.
[B.2](#A2.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition B.2 (Conditional Density ( , )). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we need the marginal density of $`U^{(i)}`$. The event
$`\{u^{(i)}\leq U^{(i)}\leq u^{(i)}+du\}`$ requires that, among the
$`K`$ i.i.d. draws, exactly $`i-1`$ fall in $`(u^{(i)}+du,1]`$, exactly
one falls in $`[u^{(i)},u^{(i)}+du)`$, and the remaining $`K-i`$ fall in
$`[0,u^{(i)})`$. The number of such assignments is the multinomial
coefficient $`\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,1!\,(K-i)!}`$, and the probability of
each assignment is
$`\left(1-u^{(i)}-du\right)^{i-1}\cdot du\cdot\left(u^{(i)}\right)^{K-i}`$.
Multiplying these terms gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(u^{(i)}\leq U^{(i)}\leq u^{(i)}+du)=\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,(K-i)!}\,(1-u^{(i)}-du)^{i-1}\cdot du\cdot(u^{(i)})^{K-i}.
``` |  | (36) |

By definition,
$`f_{U^{(i)}}(u^{(i)})=\lim_{du\to 0}\frac{P(u^{(i)}\leq U^{(i)}\leq u^{(i)}+du)}{du}`$.
Since $`(1-u^{(i)}-du)^{i-1}\to(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}`$ as $`du\to 0`$, we
obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{U^{(i)}}(u^{(i)})=\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,(K-i)!}\,(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}\,(u^{(i)})^{K-i}.
``` |  | (37) |

Applying Prop.
[B.2](#A2.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition B.2 (Conditional Density ( , )). ‣ B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle f_{U^{(i+1)}\mid U^{(i)}}(u^{(i+1)}\mid u^{(i)})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{f_{U^{(i)},U^{(i+1)}}(u^{(i)},u^{(i+1)})}{f_{U^{(i)}}(u^{(i)})}`$ |  | (38) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,(K-i-1)!}\,(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}\,(u^{(i+1)})^{K-i-1}}{\frac{K!}{(i-1)!\,(K-i)!}\,(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}\,(u^{(i)})^{K-i}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=(K-i)\,\frac{(u^{(i+1)})^{K-i-1}}{(u^{(i)})^{K-i}},`$ |  |  |

since the factors $`K!/(i-1)!`$ and $`(1-u^{(i)})^{i-1}`$ cancel. This
is precisely the density of the largest of $`K{-}i`$ i.i.d.
$`\mathcal{U}[0,u^{(i)}]`$ random variables. ∎

#### B.1.4 Generating the $`k`$ largest Gaussian random variables out of $`K`$

We now show that it is possible to generate the $`k`$ largest Gaussian
random variables out of $`K`$ via inverse transform sampling (Sec.
[B.1.1](#A2.SS1.SSS1 "B.1.1 Inverse Transform Sampling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
as follows.

Given a single uniform random variable $`U\sim\mathcal{U}[0,1]`$, one
can obtain a standard Gaussian random variable $`W=\Phi^{-1}(U)`$, where
$`\Phi`$ is the Gaussian CDF, via inverse transform sampling. Now assume
we have a sorted list of $`K`$ uniform random variables
$`U_{1}\geq U_{2}\geq...\geq U_{K}`$. Since $`\Phi`$ is a monotonically
increasing function, the largest uniform random variable, $`U_{1}`$, is
mapped to the largest Gaussian random variable, i.e.
$`\Phi^{-1}(U_{1})`$ is distributed as the largest Gaussian random
variable out of $`K`$.

##### Sampling The Largest

As shown in Prop.
[B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition B.1 (Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾). ‣ B.1.2 Distribution of the largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
the CDF of the largest uniform random variable out of $`K`$ has an
analytical solution. For $`u\in[0,1]`$, $`P(U_{1}\leq u)=u^{K}`$, hence
it can be generated via inverse transform sampling.

##### Sampling The Second Largest

Furthermore, the distribution of the second largest, conditioned on
$`U_{1}=u_{1}`$, also admits a closed-form solution (Sec.
[B.1.3](#A2.SS1.SSS3 "B.1.3 Distribution of the 𝑘 largest uniform random variable out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")):
for $`u_{2}\in[0,u_{1}]`$, it is given by
$`P(U_{2}\leq u_{2}|U_{1}=u_{1})=u_{2}^{K-1}u_{1}^{-(K-1)}`$, i.e. it is
distributed as the largest uniform variable out of $`K-1`$, supported on
$`[0,u_{1}]`$.

##### Sampling The $`k^{\text{th}}`$-largest

More generally, the same argument shows that conditioned on
$`U_{i}=u_{i}`$, the random variable $`U_{i+1}`$ is distributed as the
largest uniform variable on $`[0,u_{i}]`$ out of $`K-i+1`$. This shows
that we can sample $`U_{1},...,U_{k}`$ in decreasing order and without
simulating all the $`K`$ variables. Finally, the $`k`$ largest $`U_{i}`$
can be transformed into the $`k`$ largest standard Gaussians out of
$`K`$ as $`\{\Phi^{-1}(U_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}`$. In practice, a naive
implementation of inverse transform sampling is numerically unstable
when $`K`$ is large. For stability, operations should be implemented in
log-space. Algo.
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Sampling The 𝑘^"th"-largest ‣ B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the pseudocode of the log-space implementation.

 Input Number of variables $`N`$, standard deviation $`\sigma`$, number
of top values $`k`$

 Sample $`U_{\ell}\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)`$, for $`N\geq\ell\geq N-k+1`$

 Compute the random variables: $`R_{\ell}=\frac{\log U_{\ell}}{\ell}`$

 Compute the cumulative sums: $`P_{\ell}=\sum_{m=\ell}^{N}R_{m}`$

 Let $`V_{\ell}=\exp(P_{\ell})`$, the $`\ell`$-th sample from the
(uniform) order statistic.

 Apply inverse normal CDF: $`X^{(\ell)}=\Phi^{-1}(V_{\ell})\cdot\sigma`$

 return $`\{X^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=N}^{N-k+1}`$

Algorithm 1 Reverse Sampling from Order Statistics of Gaussian Random
Variables. Here $`N`$ corresponds to $`K{-}1`$ (the number of zero-mean
entries) and $`\sigma`$ to
$`{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}=\sqrt{1-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}`$.

#### B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling

Suppose that $`{\mathbf{x}}`$ denotes the one-hot vector of category
$`o`$. By symmetry, after applying Gaussian diffusion to
$`{\mathbf{x}}`$, all entries $`({\mathbf{x}}_{j})_{j\neq o}`$ such that
follow the exact same distribution. Therefore, they have the same
probability of being one of the top $`k`$ largest random variable.

To implement the curriculum, we must not only approximate the weights of
the embedding combination but also select which embeddings to include.
As described in Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we sample $`k`$ random indices *without repetition* excluding $`i`$. If
the random variable at position $`o`$, corresponding to the clean token,
belongs to the top-$`k`$, we replace one of the sampled indices with
$`o`$. Otherwise, we use the $`k`$ sampled indices directly.

A simple way to sample $`k`$ random indices without repetition is to
shuffle a list of $`K`$ integers and take the first $`k`$. However, this
defeats the purpose of our efficient curriculum, as it requires
materializing large tensors. Instead, Floyd’s algorithm ([Bentley,
1999](#bib.bib4)), given in Algo.
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
samples without repetition while avoiding shuffling. Although sequential
with $`k`$ iterations, it is much faster than shuffling when $`k\ll K`$.

 Input Number of possible values $`N`$, number of samples $`k`$.

 Initialize array $`S`$ of size $`k`$ to store samples

 for $`t=0`$ to $`k-1`$ do

  Sample $`j\sim\text{Randint}(0,N-k+t)`$

  if $`t>0`$ and $`j`$ appears in $`S[0:t]`$ then

   $`S[t]\leftarrow N-k+t`$ {Use largest remaining value}

  else

   $`S[t]\leftarrow j`$

  end if

 end for

 return $`S`$

Algorithm 2 Floyd’s Algorithm for Sampling Without Repetition

### B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings

After extracting the top-$`k`$ values and indices
$`(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{I})`$ from
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$, we
approximate the softmax-weighted embedding by retaining only the $`k`$
selected rows of the $`\texttt{embeddings}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times d}`$
($`d`$ denotes the embedding size):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})^{\top}\texttt{embeddings}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\exp(\mathcal{K}_{i}/\tau)}{\tilde{Z}}\texttt{embeddings}[{\mathcal{I}_{i}}],`$ |  | (39) |

where $`\texttt{embeddings}[j]`$ denotes the $`j`$-th row.

The normalizer $`\tilde{Z}`$ includes both sampled (top-$`k`$) and
unsampled terms. To account for this contribution, let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mu=\mathbb{E}[\exp(X/\tau)\mid X<\mathcal{K}_{k}]`$ |  | (40) |

denote the expectation that a r.v. $`X`$ with pmf
$`{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
is less than $`\mathcal{K}_{k}`$.

We approximate $`\tilde{Z}`$ via two cases. Let $`o`$ be the index
corresponding to the clean-token category in
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$, and let
$`\tilde{w}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$.

Case 1. If $`o`$ is not among the top-$`k`$ indices (i.e.,
$`o\notin\mathcal{I}`$, and consequently
$`\tilde{w}\notin\mathcal{K}`$), then $`\tilde{Z}`$ includes: (i) the
$`k`$ terms in $`\mathcal{K}`$, (ii) the explicit contribution from
index $`o`$, and (iii) the remaining $`K-k-1`$ unsampled terms, each
approximated by $`\mu`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tilde{Z}\approx\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{i}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{top-$k$ terms}}+\underbrace{\exp\left(\frac{\tilde{w}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{index $o$}}+\underbrace{(K-k-1)\mu}_{\text{unsampled terms}}.`$ |  | (41) |

Case 2. If $`o`$ is among the top-$`k`$ indices (i.e.,
$`o\in\mathcal{I}`$, and hence, $`\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{K}`$), its
contribution is already included in the top-$`k`$ sum, leaving $`K-k`$
unsampled terms. Hence,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tilde{Z}\approx\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{i}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{top-$k$ terms}}+\underbrace{(K-k)\mu}_{\text{unsampled terms}}.`$ |  | (42) |

Next, we derive a closed-form expression for $`\mu`$ in
([40](#A2.E40 "Equation 40 ‣ B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mu=\log\mathbb{E}[\exp(X)\mid X<\mathcal{K}_{k}]=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}{2\tau^{2}}-\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)+\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}/\tau}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right).
``` |  | (43) |

###### Proof.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mu=`$ | $`\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{X}{\tau}\right)\mid X<\mathcal{K}_{k}\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | Applying change of variables $`\bar{X}=X/\tau`$; we get, |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\mathbb{E}[\exp(\bar{X})\mid\bar{X}<\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau}\exp(x)\frac{f_{\bar{X}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(\bar{X}<\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau)}dx`$ |  |  |
|  |  | Substituting $`\sigma:={\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}/\tau`$, we get: |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left[\frac{1}{\Phi(\mathcal{K}_{k}/{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau}\exp(x)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)dx\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left[\frac{1}{\Phi(\mathcal{K}_{k}/{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+x\right)dx\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left[\frac{1}{\Phi(\mathcal{K}_{k}/{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x^{2}-2\sigma^{2}x+\sigma^{4}-\sigma^{4})\right)dx\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left[\frac{\exp(\sigma^{2}/2)}{\Phi(\mathcal{K}_{k}/{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-\sigma^{2})^{2}\right)dx\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left[\frac{\exp(\sigma^{2}/2)}{\Phi(\mathcal{K}_{k}/{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})}\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau-\sigma^{2}}{\sigma}\right)\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}-\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)+\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}/\tau-\sigma^{2}}{\sigma}\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | Substituting back $`\sigma:={\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}/\tau`$, we get: |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}{2\tau^{2}}-\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)+\log\Phi\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}/\tau}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)`$ |  | (44) |

This concludes our proof. ∎

Finally, substituting the value of $`\mu`$ from
([44](#A2.Ex6 "In Proof. ‣ B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
into
([41](#A2.E41 "Equation 41 ‣ B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and
([42](#A2.E42 "Equation 42 ‣ B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
we obtain:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tilde{Z}\approx`$ | $`\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{i}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{top-$k$ terms}}+\underbrace{\delta\exp\left(\frac{\tilde{w}}{\tau}\right)}_{\text{clean token}}+\underbrace{(K-k-\delta)\exp\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}}{2\tau^{2}}-\log\Phi\!\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)+\log\Phi\!\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}}/\tau}{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}}\right)\right)}_{\text{unsampled zero-mean terms}},`$ |  | (45) |

### B.3 Efficient computation of $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ During Training

The curriculum objective in
([9](#S2.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ Curriculum Learning ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
requires evaluating the diffusion transformation operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)`$. Directly computing
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ via
([6](#S2.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ The Diffusion Duality ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
is prohibitively expensive during training; [Sahoo et al.
(2025a)](#bib.bib7) therefore precompute and cache many
$`({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))`$
pairs, which is cumbersome. Instead, we compute
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)`$ on the fly using its
Taylor expansion, detailed below. This derivation relies on two
propositions that justify swapping the order of (i) summation and
integration (Prop.
[B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition B.5 (First Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ( ( ) , Theorem 2.25)). ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and (ii) differentiation and integration (Prop.
[B.6](#A2.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition B.6 (Second Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ( ( ) , Theorem 2.27)). ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

###### Proposition B.5 (First Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ([Folland (1999)](#bib.bib61), Theorem 2.25)).

If the sum $`\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)`$ exists for all $`x`$ and
there exists an integrable function $`g(x)`$ such that

|     |                                              |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \left|\sum_{n=0}^{k}f_{n}(x)\right|\leq g(x)
       ```                                           |     | (46) |

for all $`k`$, then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)dx=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)dx.
``` |  | (47) |

###### Proposition B.6 (Second Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ([Folland (1999)](#bib.bib61), Theorem 2.27)).

Let $`f(x,t)`$ be differentiable in $`t`$ and suppose there exists a
function $`g(x,t)`$ such that:

1.  1.
    $`\left|\frac{\partial f(x,t)}{\partial t}\right|\leq g(x,t_{0})`$
    for all $`x`$ and $`t`$ in some neighborhood
    $`|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}`$
2.  2.
    $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(x,t)dx<\infty`$ for all $`t`$

Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{d}{dt}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x,t)dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\partial f(x,t)}{\partial t}dx
``` |  | (48) |

#### B.3.1 Series Representation of $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ and $`\partial_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$

Evaluating the series expansion on the fly is faster than precomputing
and caching $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ for many
$`({\color[rgb]{0.043,0.3242,0.5898}\alpha_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}))`$
pairs as done by [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7). We can further speed
the evaluation up by fitting a low-degree polynomial to the series,
which we use in practice (see Suppl.
[B.3.2](#A2.SS3.SSS2 "B.3.2 Polynomial Approximation of 𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for the approximation error analysis). Below, we derive the series
expansion for $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ (Prop.
[B.7](#A2.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition B.7 (Series Expansion of the Diffusion Transformation Operator). ‣ B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and its time-derivative
$`\partial_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ (Prop.
[B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition B.8 (Time-Derivative of the Diffusion Transformation Operator). ‣ Advantages ‣ B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")):

###### Proposition B.7 (Series Expansion of the Diffusion Transformation Operator).

The diffusion transformation operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ can be expressed as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{t})=\frac{K}{K-1}\left[e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}M_{n}-\frac{1}{K}\right]
``` |  | (49) |

$`\nu_{t}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2}}}`$
and $`M_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$.

###### Proof.

Recall that the standard Gaussian PDF is given by

|     |                                            |     |      |
|-----|--------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \phi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}.
       ```                                         |     | (50) |

For notational convenience, let
$`\nu_{t}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2}}}`$.
We can rewrite $`\phi(x-\nu_{t})`$ in terms of $`\phi(x)`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(x-\nu_{t})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(x-\nu_{t})^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(x^{2}-2\nu_{t}x+\nu_{t}^{2})/2}=\phi(x)e^{\nu_{t}x}e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}.
``` |  | (51) |

Using the definition of the infinite series of $`e^{x}`$, we can expand
$`e^{\nu_{t}x}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(x-\nu_{t})=\phi(x)e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}x^{n}}{n!}.
``` |  | (52) |

Substituting this into our original integral:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\left(z-\nu_{t}\right)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ | $`\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(z)e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}z^{n}}{n!}\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ |  | (53) |

Since Prop.
[B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition B.5 (First Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ( ( ) , Theorem 2.25)). ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
is satisfied, as the sum is the Taylor series of the exponential
function, we can exchange the order of integration and summation. This
leads to our final result:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\left(z-\nu_{t}\right)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ | $`\displaystyle=e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ |  | (54) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}M_{n}.`$ |  |  |

∎

##### Advantages

At this point, one might ask what is gained by expressing
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ as a series expansion. There
are two key advantages. First, since
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ is intractable, [Sahoo et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib27) resort to precomputing 100k evaluations, which can
take up to two hours with the GPT-2 tokenizer. Second, they approximate
the time derivative using finite differences. Crucially, observe that
$`M_{n}`$ and $`I_{n}`$ in Prop.
[B.7](#A2.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition B.7 (Series Expansion of the Diffusion Transformation Operator). ‣ B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
and
[B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition B.8 (Time-Derivative of the Diffusion Transformation Operator). ‣ Advantages ‣ B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
are the only intractable components of the series expansion, and they
are independent of the input $`\tilde{\alpha}_{t}`$. We find that the
terms of the series decay to zero after roughly 150 terms (with slower
decay as $`t\to 1`$). Thus, instead of pre-computing 100k evaluations of
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$, it suffices to cache $`M_{n}`$
and $`I_{n}`$ for $`n<150`$. In practice, this takes only a few seconds
and can be performed at the start of training.

###### Proposition B.8 (Time-Derivative of the Diffusion Transformation Operator).

The time-derivative of the diffusion transformation operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ can be expressed as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{K\cdot e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}}{K-1}\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{\prime}}{(1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}\left[I_{n}-\nu_{t}M_{n}\right]`$ |  | (55) |

where $`\nu_{t}`$ and $`M_{n}`$ are defined as in Prop.
[B.7](#A2.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition B.7 (Series Expansion of the Diffusion Transformation Operator). ‣ B.3.1 Series Representation of 𝒯 and ∂_𝑡𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
Finally, $`I_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n+1}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$,
and $`\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{\prime}`$ denotes the time-derivative of the
Gaussian noise schedule $`\tilde{\alpha}_{t}`$.

###### Proof.

We want to compute

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{t})=\frac{K}{K-1}\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}\int\phi(z-\nu_{t})\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz.`$ |  | (56) |

To justify passing the derivative under the integral, we verify the
conditions of Prop.
[B.6](#A2.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition B.6 (Second Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ( ( ) , Theorem 2.27)). ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f(z,t)=\phi\left(z-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2}}}\right)\Phi^{K-1}(z)=\phi\left(z-\nu_{t}\right)\Phi^{K-1}(z),
``` |  | (57) |

which has time derivative

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{\partial f(z,t)}{\partial t}=\frac{(z-\nu_{t})\phi(z-\nu_{t})}{(1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}\Phi^{K-1}(z).
``` |  | (58) |

We need to find a suitable dominating function $`g`$. Let
$`1>\delta_{0}>0`$ and choose $`t_{0}=\frac{1-\delta_{0}}{2}`$. When
$`|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}`$, we have
$`t\in[t_{0}-\delta_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0}]`$. Since
$`t_{0}-\delta_{0}<t_{0}<1`$ and
$`t_{0}+\delta_{0}=\frac{1-\delta_{0}}{2}+\delta_{0}<1`$, we are
guaranteed that $`t<1`$. This ensures that $`\nu_{t}`$ is finite.
Because $`\tilde{\alpha}_{t}\in[0,1)`$ when $`t<1`$, there exists a
constant $`C`$, such that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C:=\max_{|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}}\frac{1}{(1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}<\infty.
``` |  | (59) |

For $`z\in\mathbb{R}`$ and $`|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}`$, we can bound the
absolute value of the time derivative of $`f`$ as follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial f(z,t)}{\partial t}\right|`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{|z-\nu_{t}|}{(1-\tilde{\alpha}_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}\,\phi(z-\nu_{t})\,\Phi^{K-1}(z)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq C|z-\nu_{t}|\phi(z-\nu_{t})=g(z,t).`$ |  |

Finally, for all $`t\in[0,1)`$:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(z,t)dz`$ | $`\displaystyle=C\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|z-\nu_{t}|\phi(z-\nu_{t})dz=C\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|z|\phi(z)dz`$ |  | (60) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=C\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|z|\phi(z)dz=2C\int_{0}^{\infty}z\phi(z)dz`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=2C\int_{0}^{\infty}z\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^{2}/2}dz`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{2C}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ze^{-z^{2}/2}dz`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{2C}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot 1=C\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}<\infty,`$ |  |  |

where we used the substitution $`u=z^{2}/2`$ in the integral
$`\int_{0}^{\infty}ze^{-z^{2}/2}dz`$ to obtain
$`\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-u}du=1`$. The conditions of Prop.
[B.6](#A2.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition B.6 (Second Corollary of the Dominated Convergence Theorem ( ( ) , Theorem 2.27)). ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
are satisfied, so we can pass the derivative under the integral.

Applying the derivative under the integral sign and using the identity
$`\phi(z-\nu_{t})=\phi(z)e^{\nu_{t}z}e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}`$, we have:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}\phi(z-\nu_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\phi(z)\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}[e^{\nu_{t}z-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}]`$ |  | (61) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\phi(z)e^{\nu_{t}z-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}(z-\nu_{t})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=(z-\nu_{t})\phi(z-\nu_{t})`$ |  |  |

Therefore:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{t})=\frac{K}{K-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(z-\nu_{t})\phi(z-\nu_{t})\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ |  | (62) |

Now using the Taylor series of $`\phi(z-\nu_{t})`$, found earlier, and
inverting the sum and integral as before, we find

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\nu_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{K}{K-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(z-\nu_{t})\phi(z)e^{\nu_{t}z}e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ |  | (63) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{K\cdot e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}}{K-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n+1}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz-\nu_{t}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{K\cdot e^{-\nu_{t}^{2}/2}}{K-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{n!}\left[I_{n}-\nu_{t}M_{n}\right].`$ |  |  |

where $`I_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n+1}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$ and
$`M_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{n}\phi(z)\Phi^{K-1}(z)dz`$. ∎

#### B.3.2 Polynomial Approximation of $`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$

Figure 5: Polynomial approximation and approximation error, compared to
the series approximation, truncated at 150 terms. The degree-$`9`$
polynomial (left) achieves orders of magnitude lower error than the
degree-$`5`$ polynomial (center) and sigmoid (right) approximations.

Because the Diffusion Transformation Operator
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathcal{T}}`$ has a sigmoid-like shape, we
approximate it with S-shaped functions that require only a handful of
coefficients. This allows us to store fewer parameters during training,
instead of the 100k values required by the original curriculum or the
300 coefficients from the series approximation. Concretely, we test
several functional forms with fewer than 10 parameters and fit them
using non-linear least squares, via scipy.optimize.curve_fit.

As shown in Fig.
[5](#A2.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ B.3.2 Polynomial Approximation of 𝒯 ‣ B.3 Efficient computation of 𝒯 During Training ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
approximations tend to be less accurate at the boundaries, when
$`t\approx 0`$ or $`t\approx 1`$. We find that the degree-9 polynomial
works better than a sigmoid function of the form $`a\sigma(bt+c)+d`$,
especially at the boundaries.

### B.4 Implementation of the Fast Curriculum

In Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we described our efficient curriculum. Here we provide the pseudocode
(Algo.
[3](#alg3 "Algorithm 3 ‣ B.4 Implementation of the Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))
and elaborate on the three main implementation challenges:

- •
  First, we need to sample the $`k`$ largest zero-mean Gaussian random
  variables out of $`K`$, to emulate the Gaussian Diffusion over the
  one-hot data samples $`{\mathbf{x}}`$ (Sec.
  [B.1.4](#A2.SS1.SSS4 "B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
- •
  Secondly, we must estimate the normalization constant of the softmax,
  without actually sampling the $`K`$ random variables (Sec.
  [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
- •
  Third, we require an efficient method to sample $`k`$ distinct
  integers from $`K`$ without replacement (Sec.
  [B.1.5](#A2.SS1.SSS5 "B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).

Algo.
[3](#alg3 "Algorithm 3 ‣ B.4 Implementation of the Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the pseudocode of the complete algorithm.

 Input: Clean token index $`o\in[K]`$, vocabulary size $`K`$, top-$`k`$
count $`k`$, temperature $`\tau`$, Gaussian schedule
$`({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}})`$

 Output: Approximate softmax weights $`\bm{\lambda}\in[0,1]^{k}`$ and
corresponding token indices $`\mathcal{I}\in[K]^{k}`$

  

 $`\triangleright`$ Step 1: Sample the $`k`$ largest values of
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ without
materializing all $`K`$ entries

 $`\mathcal{K}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mathcal{K}_{k}\leftarrow`$ top-$`k`$
order statistics of $`(K{-}1)`$ i.i.d.
$`{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
draws $`\triangleright`$ Algo.
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Sampling The 𝑘^"th"-largest ‣ B.1.4 Generating the 𝑘 largest Gaussian random variables out of 𝐾 ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")

 $`\tilde{w}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
$`\triangleright`$ Clean-token value: sample of
$`({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{o}`$

 $`\triangleright`$ Step 2: Build the top-$`k`$ set and approximate the
softmax normalizer $`\tilde{Z}`$

 if $`\tilde{w}>\mathcal{K}_{k}`$ then

  $`\triangleright`$ Case 2: clean token $`o`$ belongs to the top-$`k`$

  $`\mu\leftarrow\mathbb{E}[\exp(X/\tau)\mid X<\mathcal{K}_{k-1}]`$,  
$`X\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
$`\triangleright`$ Mean contribution unsimulated entry (Suppl.
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))

  $`r\leftarrow\big|\{j\in[k]:\mathcal{K}_{j}>\tilde{w}\}\big|`$
$`\triangleright`$ Rank of $`\tilde{w}`$ in $`\mathcal{K}`$

  $`\mathcal{K}\leftarrow(\mathcal{K}_{1:r},\;\tilde{w},\;\mathcal{K}_{r+1:k-1})`$
$`\triangleright`$ Insert $`\tilde{w}`$, drop the smallest
$`\mathcal{K}_{k}`$

  Sample $`k{-}1`$ indices $`\mathcal{J}`$ uniformly w/o replacement
from $`[K]\setminus\{o\}`$ $`\triangleright`$ Algo.
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")

  $`\mathcal{I}\leftarrow(\mathcal{J}_{1:r},\;o,\;\mathcal{J}_{r+1:k-1})`$

  $`\tilde{Z}\leftarrow\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp(\mathcal{K}_{i}/\tau)+(K-k)\,\mu`$
$`\triangleright`$ $`K{-}k`$ unsimulated entries, each contributing
$`\mu`$

 else

  $`\triangleright`$ Case 1: clean token $`o`$ is *not* in the top-$`k`$

  $`\mu\leftarrow\mathbb{E}[\exp(X/\tau)\mid X<\mathcal{K}_{k}]`$,  
$`X\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
$`\triangleright`$ Mean contribution unsimulated entry (Suppl.
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Approximating the Weighted Sum of the Embeddings ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"))

  Sample $`k`$ indices $`\mathcal{I}`$ uniformly w/o replacement from
$`[K]\setminus\{o\}`$ $`\triangleright`$ Algo.
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ B.1.5 Sampling Integers Without Repetitions and Without Shuffling ‣ B.1 Sampling the top-𝑘 values in 𝐰_𝑡^ℓ Without Materializing The Full Vector ‣ Appendix B Fast Curriculum ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")

  $`\tilde{Z}\leftarrow\sum_{i=1}^{k}\exp(\mathcal{K}_{i}/\tau)+(K{-}k{-}1)\,\mu+\exp(\tilde{w}/\tau)`$
$`\triangleright`$ $`\tilde{w}`$ counted exactly, $`K{-}k{-}1`$ via
$`\mu`$

 end if

 $`\triangleright`$ Step 3: Normalized softmax weights over the $`k`$
selected entries

 $`\bm{\lambda}_{i}\leftarrow\exp(\mathcal{K}_{i}/\tau)\;/\;\tilde{Z}`$,
 $`i=1,\ldots,k`$

 return $`\bm{\lambda},\;\mathcal{I}`$

Algorithm 3 Scalable Top-$`k`$ Curriculum Weights (Sec.
[4](#S4 "4 Scalable Curriculum for Faster Training ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Recall that
$`{\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}={\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}}{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}}\bm{\epsilon}`$,
$`\bm{\epsilon}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{K})`$, where
$`{\mathbf{x}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}`$ is the
one-hot vector representation of category $`o`$. Hence,
$`({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}})_{o}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\alpha}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$
and the remaining $`K{-}1`$ entries are i.i.d.
$`{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.5703,0.2188}\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}})`$.
The goal is to approximate
$`{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0}\mathrm{softmax}}({\mathbf{w}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.4688,0.4688}\ell}}/\tau)`$
using only the $`k`$ largest entries ($`k\ll K`$).

## Appendix C Experimental Details

### C.1 $`\Psi`$-samplers

#### C.1.1 OpenWebText

To evaluate the samplers, we use the pre-trained MDLM ([Sahoo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib27)) and Duo ([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7))
checkpoints, as well as their distilled variants (using SDTT
([Deschenaux and Gulcehre, 2025](#bib.bib13)) and discrete consistency
distillation, respectively, after 5 rounds of 10k steps). We re-state
the training hyperparameters of both models in Suppl.
[C.2.1](#A3.SS2.SSS1 "C.2.1 Language modeling ‣ C.2 Improved Curriculum ‣ Appendix C Experimental Details ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
For ReMDM, we use both the official implementation of [Wang et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib9) and our re-implementation, which matches the original
results while supporting additional sampling schedules beyond the
log-linear one. See Suppl.
[D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for details on selecting $`\kappa_{t}`$.

#### C.1.2 CIFAR10 (D3PM-like architecture)

| Component                         | Value     |
|-----------------------------------|-----------|
| Vocab size                        | 256       |
| Number of ResNet blocks per scale | 2         |
| Base channels                     | 128       |
| Channel multiplier per scale      | (1,2,2,2) |
| Attention resolutions             | 16        |
| Conditional embedding dimension   | 128       |
| Number of parameters              | 35.8M     |

Table 3: Model architecture on CIFAR10

We train a U-Net backbone ([Ronneberger et al., 2015](#bib.bib31)) for
1.5M steps with a batch size of 128, using class conditioning with a
class-dropout rate of 0.1 (as in [Schiff et al. (2025)](#bib.bib10)),
and the default hyperparameters of [Austin et al. (2023)](#bib.bib16)
(Table
[3](#A3.T3 "Table 3 ‣ C.1.2 CIFAR10 (D3PM-like architecture) ‣ C.1 Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix C Experimental Details ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
For both MDLM and Duo, we experiment with time-conditional and
unconditional variants, and train models using either cosine or
log-linear noise schedules. See Table
[6](#A4.T6 "Table 6 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for the ancestral-sampling evaluation of all variants after
pre-training. See Suppl.
[D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
for details on selecting $`\kappa_{t}`$.

### C.2 Improved Curriculum

#### C.2.1 Language modeling

We adopt the same setup as prior work on discrete diffusion ([Lou et
al., 2024](#bib.bib28); [Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)), and restate it for completeness.

##### LM1B

We detokenize the One Billion Words ([Chelba et al., 2014](#bib.bib25))
as in [Lou et al. (2024)](#bib.bib28); [Sahoo et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib27)¹¹ 1
[https://github.com/louaaron/Score-Entropy-Discrete-Diffusion/blob/main/data.py](https://github.com/louaaron/Score-Entropy-Discrete-Diffusion/blob/main/data.py),
and tokenize it using the bert-base-uncased tokenizer ([Devlin et al.,
2019](#bib.bib21)), as [He et al. (2022)](#bib.bib32). We use a context
length of 128 and pad shorter documents.

##### OpenWebText

We tokenize OpenWebText ([Gokaslan and Cohen, 2019](#bib.bib26)) with
the GPT-2 tokenizer, concatenate sequences to a length of 1024, and
insert an eos token between documents. Since the dataset lacks an
official validation split, we reserve the last 100k documents for
validation.

##### Backbone

We parameterize all models using the modified diffusion transformer
architecture of [Peebles and Xie (2023)](#bib.bib39), following [Lou et
al. (2024)](#bib.bib28); [Sahoo et al. (2024)](#bib.bib27). Our models
use 12 layers, a hidden dimension of 768, 12 attention heads, and a
timestep embedding of size 128 for the uniform-state diffusion variants.
Word embeddings are not tied between input and output.

##### Curriculum Lookup

For the Duo baseline, we train models using the original code. To
implement the efficient curriculum, we replace the full linear
combination of embeddings by a sparse lookup, implemented using
torch.nn.functional.embedding_bag to avoid materializing intermediate
tensors. The curriculum phase lasts for the first 500k steps, after
which we perform regular embedding table lookups, just like [Sahoo et
al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7).

##### Optimization

We train all models with the AdamW optimizer ([Loshchilov and Hutter,
2019](#bib.bib29)) using a batch size of 512. The learning rate is
linearly warmed up from 0 to $`3\times 10^{-4}`$ over 2,500 steps, then
kept constant for the remainder of training. We apply a dropout rate of
0.1 throughout.

### C.3 Downstream Evaluation Protocol

We evaluate downstream performance using the lm-eval-harness library
([Gao et al., 2024](#bib.bib56)), following the protocol of [Deschenaux
et al. (2025)](#bib.bib55). We focus on multiple choice tasks, where the
log-likelihood of each candidate answer, given a prompt, is computed and
the answer with the highest score is selected. For diffusion language
models, which optimize a variational bound on the log-likelihood of the
full sequence, we adapt the evaluation by using Bayes’ rule:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log p({\mathbf{y}}_{i}|{\mathbf{x}})=\log p({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}_{i})-\log p({\mathbf{x}})\propto\log p({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}_{i}),
``` |  | (64) |

Since $`\log p({\mathbf{x}})`$ does not depend on the candidate
$`{\mathbf{y}}_{i}`$, we simply select the answer that maximizes
$`\log p({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}_{i})`$. In practice, we use the
log-likelihood ELBO
([4](#S2.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 2.1.2 Uniform-state Diffusion Processes ‣ 2.1 Discrete Diffusion Models ‣ 2 Background ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")),
estimated via Monte Carlo with 1024 samples, and choose the continuation
$`{\mathbf{y}}_{i}`$ with the highest estimated likelihood.

### C.4 Zero-Shot Likelihood

Our setting is the same as used by [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7).
Specifically, we measure the likelihood of the models trained on
OpenWebText using the validation splits of seven diverse datasets: Penn
Tree Bank (PTB; [Marcus et al. (1993)](#bib.bib46)), Wikitext ([Merity
et al., 2016](#bib.bib50)), One Billion Words (LM1B; [Chelba et al.
(2014)](#bib.bib25)), Lambada ([Paperno et al., 2016](#bib.bib51)), AG
News ([Zhang et al., 2016](#bib.bib52)), and Scientific Papers (Pubmed
and Arxiv subsets; [Cohan et al. (2018)](#bib.bib54)). The datasets are
detokenized following the protocol of [Lou et al. (2024)](#bib.bib28);
[Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7). We wrap all sequences to a maximum
length of 1024 tokens and do not insert eos tokens between them. Table
[5](#A4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows that we reach similar performance as Duo.

## Appendix D Additional Experimental results

In Suppl.
[D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Tuning 𝜅_𝑡 for the Ψ-samplers ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we elaborate on the impact of $`\kappa_{t}`$ on the performance of the
$`\Psi`$-samplers. In Suppl.
[D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Distribution of the top 𝑘 entries of the softmax ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
we show that our efficient curriculum produces weights with the same
marginal distributions as [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7).

### D.1 Tuning $`\kappa_{t}`$ for the $`\Psi`$-samplers

Figure 6: $`\Psi`$-samplers, which generalize ReMDM, significantly
improve the Inception Score on CIFAR-10, compared to ancestral sampling.

As discussed in Sec.
[5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Ψ-Samplers ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
the choice of $`\kappa_{t}`$ is critical for strong performance. With a
poor choice of $`\kappa_{t}`$, $`\Psi`$-samplers can underperform
ancestral sampling. Below, we report all of our hyperparameter sweeps
across datasets.

- •
  We perform image modeling on CIFAR-10 using the U-Net architecture of
  [Austin et al. (2023)](#bib.bib16); [Schiff et al.
  (2025)](#bib.bib10), and use horizontal flipping as the sole data
  augmentation.
- •
  We evaluate $`\Psi`$-samplers on OpenWebText ([Gokaslan and Cohen,
  2019](#bib.bib26)) using the original checkpoint of MDLM ([Sahoo et
  al., 2024](#bib.bib27)) and Duo ([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7)).

#### D.1.1 CIFAR-10

We report FID ([Heusel et al., 2018](#bib.bib44)), computed between 50k
generated samples and the training set. Before evaluating
$`\Psi`$-samplers, we ablate on the training hyperparameters.
Specifically, we train models with cosine and log-linear noise schedule,
optionally with time-conditioning. We sample with both cosine and
log-linear schedules. Finally, we check whether nucleus sampling
([Holtzman et al., 2020](#bib.bib41)) and greedy decoding on the final
step can help, compared to vanilly ancestral sampling. Since nucleus
sampling helps Duo but not MDLM, we compare the two models without
nucleus sampling. Table
[6](#A4.T6 "Table 6 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the validation perplexity and FID for a few number of sampling
steps. Table
[7](#A4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
reports FID for ancestral sampling using step counts that are powers of
two, from 32 up to 4096. Table
[8](#A4.T8 "Table 8 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the results with ReMDM. Table
[9](#A4.T9 "Table 9 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
reports FID scores for $`\Psi`$-samplers using a stepwise-constant
$`\kappa`$ schedule. Table
[11](#A4.T11 "Table 11 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the performance of $`\Psi`$-samplers using the $`\kappa`$ schedule
equivalent to ReMDM. We obtain similar results, which supports our
theoretical claims.

- •
  MDLM (Ancestral). Training with cosine noise schedule and time
  conditioning yields the best validation perplexity and FID.
- •
  MDLM (ReMDM). We find that ReMDM improves the best FID over ancestral
  sampling, from 24.73 to 23.71 using 4096 sampling steps. Nucleus
  sampling can help at very low step counts, but the best performance is
  obtained with ancestral sampling. As the number of steps increases,
  nucleus sampling *worsens* the FID.
- •
  Duo (Ancestral). Cosine training without time conditioning yields the
  lowest perplexity, while log-linear training without time conditioning
  gives the best FID. We use the latter in downstream experiments.
  Nucleus sampling improves FID, and greedy decoding slightly worsens
  it.
- •
  Duo ($`\Psi`$-samplers). $`\Psi`$-samplers further improve performance
  beyond ReMDM. With the log-linear sampling schedule (as used by
  ReMDM), $`\Psi`$-samplers reduce the FID from 23.71 to 20.71. Using a
  cosine sampling schedule further improves the FID. Overall, Duo
  improves *from an FID of 25.63 (ancestral) to 15.05* with
  $`\Psi`$-samplers, and MDLM improves from *24.73 (ancestral) to 17.86*
  with $`\Psi`$-samplers.

#### D.1.2 OpenWebText

We report the generative perplexity using GPT-2 Large, following
standard practice ([Sahoo et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Sahoo et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib7)). Because language models can artificially lower the
generative perplexity by producing repetitive text, we also report
unigram entropy ([Dieleman et al., 2022](#bib.bib62)), as a proxy.

Some $`\Psi`$-samplers schedules reduce the unigram entropy more than
others. Therefore, for figures, we select the $`\kappa`$ schedule whose
unigram entropy matches (or is closest to) the entropy of samples
generated with ancestral sampling. If multiple schedules achieve the
same entropy, we choose the one with the lowest generative perplexity.
We indicate which schedule is used for plots by highlighting the
corresponding row in blue in the tables. Overall, the $`\Psi`$-samplers
can reduce the Gen. PPL of *all* models while retaining the unigram
entropy. Best results are achieved using the rescale schedule with
$`\eta\in\{0.01,0.02\}`$, for both MDLM and Duo.

Table
[12](#A4.T12 "Table 12 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the generative perplexity of MDLM and Duo after pre-training and
after distillation with SDTT ([Deschenaux and Gulcehre,
2025](#bib.bib13)) or DCD ([Sahoo et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7))
respectively, with and without nucleus sampling, using ancestral
sampling. Table
[13](#A4.T13 "Table 13 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the results when sampling with $`\Psi`$-samplers that are
equivalent to ReMDM ([Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)), with the
*non-distilled* models, while Table
[14](#A4.T14 "Table 14 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
shows the result for the distilled models.

### D.2 Distribution of the top $`k`$ entries of the softmax

To verify that our sparse implementation accurately approximates the
curriculum weights of [Sahoo et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7), we compare the
empirical distributions of the top-$`k`$ largest entries between the
original and our efficient implementation. While matching marginal
distributions does not guarantee matching joint distributions, matching
marginals are necessary for matching joints, and are easier to
visualize. Recall that experimentally, our efficient implementation is
sufficient to achieve strong performance (Sec.
[5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Fast Curriculum ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
Specifically, we show histograms using a tokenizer with $`100k`$ tokens
in Figures
[7](#A4.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[8](#A4.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[9](#A4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[10](#A4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
and with the GPT-2 tokenizer in Figures
[11](#A4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[12](#A4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[13](#A4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
[14](#A4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers"),
with varying temperature and log signal-to-noise ratios. In all cases,
the top $`k`$ variables have matching distributions.

Table 9: FID on CIFAR-10 with $`\Psi`$-samplers, where $`\Psi`$-samplers
are activated for steps with $`t\in[t_{\text{off}},t_{\text{on}}]`$,
when $`\kappa_{t}`$ is kept constant (according to the $`\kappa`$
column, $`1`$ otherwise). We use the same checkpoints as in Table
[7](#A4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers").
Using a cosine sampling schedule and light noise injection ($`\kappa`$
close to 1) generally perform best. The CIFAR-10 curves in Fig.
[1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")
show the best FID per number of steps.

Table 11: FID on CIFAR-10 using $`\Psi`$-samplers whose $`\kappa_{t}`$
schedulers are equivalent to ReMDM. We use no nucleus sampling, no
temperature scaling, and $`\mathrm{cfg}=1`$. As expected, with the
log-linear scheduler, we reach a similar FID as when using the ReMDM
codebase (Table
[8](#A4.T8 "Table 8 ‣ D.3 Training Efficiency of Our Fast Curriculum ‣ Appendix D Additional Experimental results ‣ The Diffusion Duality, Chapter II: Ψ-Samplers")).
However, note that by using a log-linear scheduler, using a constant
$`\kappa_{t}=0.99`$, we reach a better FID than with the original ReMDM
scheduler.

Table 12: Generative Perplexity (Gen. PPL) and Unigram Entropy on
OpenWebText ([Gokaslan and Cohen, 2019](#bib.bib26)) with ancestral
sampling (no nucleus, no temperature scaling). We train using the
log-linear noise scheduler, and sampling with the cosine scheduler is
slightly better. We stick to the log-linear schedule for sampling in
further experiments, to follow prior work, and since the cosine schedule
only marginally reduces the Gen. PPL.
````
